\displaystyle=(-1)^{N}\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{W}(y)}z^{|\omega|}Z[0,|\omega|]J^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}[0,|\omega|]{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{x-{e}_{\kappa}\not\in S^{\omega}_{|\omega|}\}},

(3.27)

\displaystyle\Pi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota,\kappa}_{z}(x)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{N}\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{W}(x)}z^{|\omega|+1}Z[0,|\omega|]J^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}[0,|\omega|]{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{x-{e}_{\kappa}\not\in S_{|\omega|}^{\omega}\}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}^{\Pi}_{\iota}(\omega,x,\kappa),

(3.28)

to be the restrictions of the NoBLE coefficients to laces of fixed size, with the convention that
J^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})}[0,|\omega|]=\delta_{0,|\omega|}
.

The dominant contributions of these coefficients are given by

\displaystyle\bar{\Xi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})}}_{z}(0)=

\displaystyle\bar{G}_{z}(0)=g_{z},\quad\bar{\Psi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\kappa}_{z}(0)=\bar{G}^{\iota}_{z}(0)=g^{\iota}_{z},\quad\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{z}(0)=\bar{G}_{z}({e}_{\iota}).

(3.29)

We use these terms explicitly in our analysis. We perform the NoBLE analysis using the following coefficients:

\displaystyle\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}}_{z}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{g_{z}}\big(\bar{\Xi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}}_{z}(x)-\delta_{0,x}\delta_{0,N}\bar{\Xi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})}}_{z}(0)\big),\qquad\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota}_{z}(x)=\frac{1}{g_{z}}\bar{\Xi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota}_{z}(x),

(3.30)

\displaystyle\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\kappa}_{z}(0)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{g^{\iota}_{z}}\big(\bar{\Psi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\kappa}_{z}(x)-\delta_{0,x}\delta_{0,N}\bar{\Psi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\kappa}_{z}(0)\big),

(3.31)

\displaystyle\Xi_{z}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{N}\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}}_{z}(x),\qquad\Xi^{\iota}_{z}(x)=\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{N}\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota}_{z}(x),

(3.32)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\kappa}_{z}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{N}\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\kappa}_{z}(x),\qquad\Pi^{\iota,\kappa}_{z}(x)=\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{N}\Pi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota,\kappa}_{z}(x),

(3.33)

where we explicitly subtract the dominant contributions, of which most of them are also present for the NBW. The situation simplifies considerably for LTs, due to the absence of double connections, so that

\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{z}(x)=\delta_{0,x}G_{z}({e}_{\iota}),\qquad\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})}}_{z}(x)=\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{z}(x)=0,

(3.34)

while, using (
3.16
), as well as (
3.20
)–(
3.21
),

\Pi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota,\kappa}_{z}(x)=\delta_{0,x}z\sum_{T\colon 0\in T}z^{|T|}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{x-{e}_{\kappa}\not\in T\}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{{e}_{\iota}\in T\}}.

(3.35)

For LAs, several further contributions due to
x\neq 0
, which require a double connection, arise.
Using the above notation we obtain (
2.7
)-(
2.8
) by dividing the equations (
3.14
) and (
3.22
) by
g_{z}
. Thereby, we have completed the proof of Proposition
2.1
and have identified the NoBLE coefficients appearing in it.
∎

3.3
Split for the coefficients

In this section, we define further terms that we extract from the NoBLE coefficients to be used in the analysis of
[
12
]
. By extracting these terms we reduce the size of the perturbation, which ultimately improves the numerical accuracy of our analysis. We give first the definition for LAs and then explain how terms simplify for LTs.

Definition of the explicit terms for
N=0
.

For a LA
A
and
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we denote by
x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle A}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}y
the event that
x
and
y
are doubly connected via bonds in
A
. By
d_{A}(x,y)
we denote the intrinsic distance of
x,y
in
A
, meaning the length of the shortest connection of
x
and
y
using bonds in
A
. For
N=0
, we define

\displaystyle\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})}}_{\alpha,z}(x)

\displaystyle=(1-\delta_{0,x})\frac{1}{g_{z}}\sum_{A\colon 0,x\in A}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle A}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}x\}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{d_{A}(0,x)=1\}}z^{|A|},

(3.36)

\displaystyle\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\kappa}_{\alpha,{\scriptscriptstyle I},z}(x)

\displaystyle=(1-\delta_{0,x})\frac{1}{g^{\iota}_{z}}\sum_{A\colon 0,x\in A}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle A}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}x\}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{x-{e}_{\kappa}\not\in A\}}

(3.37)

\displaystyle\qquad\times({\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{d_{A}(0,x)=1\}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{x={e}_{\kappa}\}}+{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{|{e}_{\kappa}-x|=1\}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{d_{A}(0,x)=2\}}),

\displaystyle\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\kappa}_{\alpha,{\scriptscriptstyle II},z}(x)

\displaystyle=(1-\delta_{0,x})\frac{1}{g^{\iota}_{z}}\sum_{A\colon 0,x\in A}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle A}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}x\}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{d_{A}(0,x)=1\}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{x-{e}_{\kappa}\not\in A\}},

(3.38)

and

\displaystyle\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{\alpha,{\scriptscriptstyle I},z}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{g_{z}}\sum_{A\colon 0,x,{e}_{\iota}\in A}z^{|A|}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle A}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}x\}}\left(\delta_{0,x}+\delta_{{e}_{\iota},x}+(1-\delta_{0,x}){\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{d_{A}({e}_{\iota},x)=1\}}\right),

(3.39)

\displaystyle\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{\alpha,{\scriptscriptstyle II},z}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{g_{z}}\sum_{A\colon 0,x,{e}_{\iota}\in A}z^{|A|}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle A}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}x\}}\left(\delta_{0,x}+\delta_{{e}_{\iota},x}+(1-\delta_{{e}_{\iota},x}){\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{d_{A}(0,x)=1\}}\right),

(3.40)

\displaystyle\Pi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota,\kappa}_{\alpha,z}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{A\colon 0,x,{e}_{\iota}\in A}z^{|A|+1}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle A}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}x\}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{x-{e}_{\kappa}\not\in A\}}\left(\delta_{{e}_{\iota},x}+\delta_{{e}_{\iota}+{e}_{\kappa},x}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{d_{A}({e}_{\iota}+{e}_{\kappa},{e}_{\iota})=1\}}\right).

(3.41)

For
N=1
, the terms are more involved and are given by

\displaystyle\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}}_{\alpha,z}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{g_{z}}\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{W}(x)}z^{|\omega|}Z[0,|\omega|]J^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}}[0,|\omega|]{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{\underline{b}^{\omega}_{0}=0\}}

(3.42)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\delta_{0,x}+\delta_{|\omega|,1}\delta_{\overline{b}^{\omega}_{0},x}+{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{x\in S_{0}\cap S_{|\omega|}\}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{d_{S_{0}}(0,x)=1\}}+{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{0\in S_{0}\cap S_{|\omega|}\not\ni x\}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{d_{S_{|\omega|}(0,x)=1}\}}\right),

\displaystyle\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})},\kappa}_{\alpha,{\scriptscriptstyle II},z}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{g^{\iota}_{z}}\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{W}(x)}z^{|\omega|}Z[0,|\omega|]J^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}}[0,|\omega|]{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{\underline{b}^{\omega}_{0}=0\}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{x-{e}_{\kappa}\not\in S_{|\omega|}^{\omega}\}}

(3.43)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\delta_{0,x}+\delta_{|\omega|,1}\delta_{\overline{b}^{\omega}_{0},x}+{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{x\in S_{0}\cap S_{|\omega|}\}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{d_{S_{0}}(0,x)=1\}}+{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{0\in S_{0}\cap S_{|\omega|}\not\ni x\}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{d_{S_{|\omega|}(0,x)=1}\}}\right),

\displaystyle\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})},\kappa}_{\alpha,{\scriptscriptstyle I},z}(x)

\displaystyle=(\delta_{0,x}+\delta_{x,{e}_{\kappa}})\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})},\kappa}_{\alpha,{\scriptscriptstyle II},z}(x)

(3.44)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{2dD(x-{e}_{\kappa})}{g^{\iota}_{z}}\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{W}(x)}z^{|\omega|}Z[0,|\omega|]J^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}}[0,|\omega|]{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{x-{e}_{\kappa}\not\in S_{|\omega|}^{\omega}\}}\delta_{|\omega|,2}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{\underline{b}^{\omega}_{0}=0\}}\delta_{\overline{b}^{\omega}_{0},\underline{b}^{\omega}_{1}}\delta_{\overline{b}^{\omega}_{1},x}.

All sausage-walks contributing to these sums have the following in common: (a) The first step of the backbone
\underline{b}^{\omega}_{0}
starts at the origin.
(b) The sausages
S_{0}
and
S_{|\omega|}
intersect either at
0
or at
x
. (c) At least one bond is explicitly present. Most of the times this bond is
(0,x)
, forced by
d_{A}(0,x)=1
.

We define the remainder terms arising through this split by

\displaystyle\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R},z}

\displaystyle=\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})}}_{z}-\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})}}_{\alpha,z},\qquad\qquad\quad\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R},z}=\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}}_{z}-\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}}_{\alpha,z},

(3.45)

\displaystyle\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R,I},z}

\displaystyle=\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{z}-\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{\alpha,{\scriptscriptstyle I},z},\qquad\qquad\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R,II},z}=\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{z}-\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{\alpha,{\scriptscriptstyle II},z},

(3.46)

\displaystyle\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})},\iota}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R,I},z}

\displaystyle=\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})},\iota}_{z}-\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})},\iota}_{\alpha,{\scriptscriptstyle I},z},\qquad\qquad\ \Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})},\iota}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R,II},z}=\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})},\iota}_{z}-\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})},\iota}_{\alpha,{\scriptscriptstyle II},z},

(3.47)

\displaystyle\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R,I},z}

\displaystyle=\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{z}-\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{\alpha,{\scriptscriptstyle I},z},\qquad\qquad\ \ \Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R,II},z}=\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{z}-\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{\alpha,{\scriptscriptstyle II},z},

(3.48)

\displaystyle\Pi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota,\kappa}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R},z}

\displaystyle=\Pi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota,\kappa}_{z}-\Pi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota,\kappa}_{\alpha,z}.

(3.49)

This completes the necessary split for LAs.

Lattice trees.

We use the same definitions for LTs, where we sum over LTs
T
instead of LAs
A
.
However, for LTs the terms simplify considerably, as double connections are not possible. This is especially true for
N=0
, for which, for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
(recall (
3.34
)),

\displaystyle\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})}}_{\alpha,z}(x)=\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{\alpha,{\scriptscriptstyle I},z}(x)=\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{\alpha,{\scriptscriptstyle II},z}(x)=0.

(3.50)

Further, the split actually captures the complete contribution of
\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{z}
, since

\displaystyle\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{z}(x)=\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{\alpha,{\scriptscriptstyle I},z}(x)

\displaystyle=\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{\alpha,{\scriptscriptstyle II},z}(x)=\delta_{0,x}\frac{\bar{G}_{z}({e}_{\iota})}{g_{z}}=\delta_{0,x}G_{z}({e}_{\iota}).

(3.51)

This completes the derivation of the split of the coefficients as used in
[
12
, Section 4]
.

3.4
Assumptions on the model

In this section, we verify most of the assumptions necessary to apply the analysis of
[
12
]
.
We start by proving the assumption that are independent of the NoBLE:
[
12
, Assumptions 2.2, 2.3 and 2.4]
.

3.4.1
Assumption on the two-point function

We begin with
[
12
, Assumption 2.4]
as it will help us prove
[
12
, Assumptions 2.2]
.

[
12
, Assumption 2.4]
:

For
z\in[0,z_{c})
, the functions
z\mapsto\bar{\mu}_{z}
and
z\mapsto\mu_{z}
are continuous.

To verify this assumption, we choose
\bar{\mu}_{z}=zg_{z}
and
\mu_{z}=zg_{z}^{\iota}
, with

\displaystyle g^{\iota}_{z}=\sum_{A\colon A\ni 0}z^{|A|}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad g^{\iota}_{z}=\sum_{A\colon{e}_{1}\not\in A\ni 0}z^{|A|}.

(3.52)

By Abel’s Theorem, the one-point function is continuous within the radius of convergence of these power series, which is at most
z_{c}
, see (
1.3
) and the text thereafter. Thus, also
z\mapsto\bar{\mu}_{z}
and
z\mapsto\mu_{z}
are continuous.

[
12
, Assumption 2.2]
:

There exists a
z_{I}\in[0,z_{c})
such that

\displaystyle G_{z}(x)\leq B_{1/(2d-1)}(x)=\frac{2d-2}{2d-1}C_{1/2d}(x)

(3.53)

for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
z\in[0,z_{I}]
.

To prove this statement for LTs and LAs we adapt an argument used in
[
18
, Proof of Lemma 3.1]
.
We know that each LT/LA containing
0
and
x
contains a path of bonds that connects
0
and
x
. Each point of the path is connected to at most one rib/sausage.
The weight of all possible rib/sausages
z^{|S_{i}|}
(see (
3.2
)) can be bounded by
g_{z}
, as each rib/sausage
S_{i}
is itself a LT/LA.
For
x\neq 0
we can improve this by bounding the weight by
g^{\iota}_{z}
instead. This is possible as each rib/sausage needs to avoid at least the next/previous step of the path from
0
to
x
. From this, we conclude for
x\neq 0
that

\displaystyle\bar{G}_{z}(x)\leq g^{\iota}_{z}\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{W}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\rm NBW}}}(x)}(zg^{\iota}_{z})^{|\omega|}=g^{\iota}_{z}B_{zg^{\iota}_{z}}(x),

(3.54)

where
\mathcal{W}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\rm NBW}}}(x)
is the set of all NBWs (see the text above (
2.5
)) starting at
0
and ending at
x
, and
|\omega|
is the number of steps of the NBW
\omega
. The inequality (
3.54
) then follows for all
z
for which
zg^{\iota}_{z}\leq(2d-1)^{-1}
and
x\neq 0
. We define

\displaystyle z_{I}:=\sup\Big\{z\colon zg_{z}^{\iota}\leq\frac{1}{2d-1}\Big\}.

(3.55)

This is well defined as
\mu_{z}=zg_{z}^{\iota}
is continuous and non-decreasing in
z
with
zg_{z}^{\iota}=0
when
z=0
. To complete the proof of
[
12
, Assumption 2.2]
, we still need to prove that
z_{I}<z_{c}
.
For this we note that
g_{z}-g_{z}^{\iota}=\bar{G}_{z}({e}_{1})
and use (
3.54
) to obtain

\displaystyle\bar{\chi}(z)=\sum_{x}\bar{G}_{z}(x)\leq\bar{G}_{z}(0)+\sum_{x\neq 0}g^{\iota}_{z}B_{zg^{\iota}_{z}}(x)\leq g^{\iota}_{z}\big(1+B_{zg^{\iota}_{z}}({e}_{1})+\chi^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm NBW}(zg^{\iota}_{z})\big).

(3.56)

From this we conclude that
z_{I}\leq z_{c}
as otherwise
\chi(z_{c})=\infty
, while
\chi^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm NBW}(z_{c}g_{z_{c}}^{\iota})
on the right-hand side is finite.
To exclude that
z_{I}=z_{c}
, we see that (
3.54
) also holds when we replace
g^{\iota}_{z}
with

\displaystyle g^{\prime}_{z}=\min_{\kappa\neq 1}\sum_{A:{e}_{1},{e}_{\kappa}\not\in A\ni 0}z^{|A|}

(3.57)

for all ribs/sausages except those at
0
and
x
. We define
z^{\prime}
as the value
z
such that
z^{\prime}g^{\prime}_{z^{\prime}}=(2d-1)^{-1}
, and conclude as in (
3.56
) that
z_{c}\geq z^{\prime}>z_{I}
.
Recalling
\bar{G}_{z}(x)=g_{z}G_{z}(x)
and
g^{\iota}_{z}<g_{z}
we conclude (
3.54
) from (
3.53
). This concludes the proof of
[
12
, Assumption 2.4]
.

Additionally, we prove the lower bound
z_{I}\geq(2d-1)^{-1}{\mathrm{e}}^{-1}
. As this uses ideas not used elsewhere, we move the proof to Appendix
A
(see Lemma
A.1
).

[
12
, Assumption 2.3]
: Growth of the two-point function.

We need to show that for every
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, the two-point functions
z\mapsto G_{z}(x)
and
z\mapsto G^{\iota}_{z}(x)

are non-decreasing and differentiable in
z\in(0,z_{c})
. Further, we need to show that for all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a constant
c_{\varepsilon}\geq 0
such that for all
z\in(0,z_{c}-\varepsilon)
and
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dz}G_{z}(x)\leq c_{\varepsilon}(G_{z}\star D\star G_{z})(x),\quad\text{ and therefore }\quad\frac{d}{dz}\hat{G}_{z}(0)\leq c_{\varepsilon}\hat{G}_{z}(0)^{2}.

(3.58)

Finally, we need to show that for each
z\in(0,z_{c})
, there exists a constant
K(z)<\infty
such that
\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|x|^{2}G_{z}(x)<K(z)
. We will do this now.

As a generating function of a non-negative sequence (see (
1.2
)), the two-point function is clearly non-decreasing in the parameter
z

as well as differentiable in
z
for
z\in(0,z_{c})
. Next, we first prove the bound on the derivative in (
3.58
) for LTs.
We know that a LT
T
with
|T|
edges contains
|T|+1
vertices. We use this property to compute for
x\neq 0

\displaystyle\sum_{T\ni 0,x}\frac{d}{dz}z^{|T|}=\sum_{T\ni 0,x}{|T|}z^{|T|-1}=\sum_{y\neq 0}\sum_{T\ni 0,x,y}z^{|T|-1}.

(3.59)

As a LT
T\ni x,y
contains no loops, the path from
x
to
y
is unique. We denote this path by
b^{T}(x,y)
.
By
u
we denote the last vertex that the paths from
0
to
x
and from
0
to
y
have in common.
For
u\neq x
we split the walk at
u
into three individual trees and bound the contributions of these individual trees by two-point functions.
Doing this, we have to take into account that in (
3.59
) the tree
T
is only weighted by
z^{|T|-1}
,
so that we have to choose one bond of the tree that does not carry the weight
z
.
We choose the first step of the path from
u
to
x
to be this bond.
For
u=x
there exists no first step. In this case, we choose the last step of the path from
0
to
x
to be the bond without weight
z
. Using that
\bar{G}_{z}(0)\geq 1
, we obtain the bound

\displaystyle\sum_{T\ni 0,x}\frac{d}{dz}z^{|T|}\leq

\displaystyle\ \sum_{y\neq 0}\sum_{u\neq x,v}\bar{G}^{(t)}_{z}(u)2dD(v-u)\bar{G}^{(t)}_{z}(x-v)\bar{G}^{(t)}_{z}(u-y)

(3.60)

\displaystyle\ +\sum_{y\neq 0}\sum_{v}\bar{G}^{(t)}_{z}(v)2dD(v-x)\bar{G}^{(t)}_{z}(x-y)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ 4d(\bar{G}^{(t)}_{z}\star D\star\bar{G}^{(t)}_{z})(x)\sum_{y}\bar{G}^{(t)}_{z}(y)=4d\hat{\bar{G}}^{(t)}_{z}(0)(\bar{G}^{(t)}_{z}\star D\star\bar{G}^{(t)}_{z})(x).

For the normalized two-point function, we conclude

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dz}G^{(t)}_{z}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dz}\frac{\bar{G}^{(t)}_{z}(x)}{g^{(t)}_{z}}=\frac{1}{g^{(t)}_{z}}\sum_{T\ni 0,x}\frac{d}{dz}z^{|T|}-\frac{\bar{G}^{(t)}_{z}(x)}{(g^{(t)}_{z})^{2}}\sum_{T\ni 0}\frac{d}{dz}z^{|T|}

\displaystyle\leq 4d(g^{(t)}_{z})^{2}\hat{G}^{(t)}_{z}(0)(G^{(t)}_{z}\star D\star G^{(t)}_{z})(x),

(3.61)

as required. To conclude such an inequality for LAs, we note that an animal with
|A|
bonds contains at least
|A|/d
vertices and as for the LT compute that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dz}G^{(a)}_{z}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{g^{(a)}_{z}}\sum_{A\ni 0,x}|A|z^{|A|-1}\leq\frac{d}{g^{(a)}_{z}}\sum_{A\ni 0,x,v}z^{|A|-1}

\displaystyle\leq 4d^{2}(g^{(a)}_{z})^{2}\hat{G}^{(a)}_{z}(0)(G^{(a)}_{z}\star D\star G^{(a)}_{z})(x).

(3.62)

As the last step we prove that for all
z<z_{c}
there exists
K(z)<\infty
such that

\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|x|^{2}G_{z}(x)<K(z)
. In
[
20
, (1.1)]
, it is proved that the connectivity constants
\lambda=1/z_{c}
can be used to prove that

\displaystyle t_{n}(0)\leq\lambda^{n}(n+1).

(3.63)

Let
|x|_{\infty}:=\max_{i=1}^{d}|x_{i}|
be the supremum norm and compute

\displaystyle\bar{G}_{z}(x)=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=|x|_{\infty}}^{\infty}t_{n}(x)z^{n}\leq\sum_{n=|x|_{\infty}}^{\infty}t_{n}(0)z^{n}\leq\sum_{n=|x|_{\infty}}^{\infty}(z/z_{c})^{n}(n+1).

(3.64)

From this we conclude that
\bar{G}_{z}(x)
decays exponentially, i.e., there exists
c,m(z)\in(0,\infty)
such that

\displaystyle\bar{G}_{z}(x)\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{n=|x|_{\infty}}^{\infty}(n+1)(z/z_{c})^{n}\leq c{\mathrm{e}}^{-m(z)|x|_{\infty}}.

(3.65)

We use this bound to conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|x|^{2}\bar{G}_{z}(x)\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|x|^{2}c{\mathrm{e}}^{-m(z)|x|_{\infty}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle|x|^{2}\leq d^{2}|x|_{\infty}^{2}}}{{\leq}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(dn)^{2}c{\mathrm{e}}^{-m(z)n}\sum_{x\colon|x|_{\infty}=n}1

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ d^{3}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{d+1}c{\mathrm{e}}^{-m(z)n}:=K(z)<\infty,

(3.66)

which proves the desired statement.
∎

3.4.2
Assumptions on the NoBLE-coefficients

In this section, we verify the assumptions on the NoBLE coefficients formulated in
[
12
, Assumptions 4.1, 4.2 and 4.4]
.

[
12
, Definition 2.5]
Symmetry of the model.

We denote by
\mathcal{P}_{d}
the set of all permutations of
\{1,2,\dots,d\}
. For
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{d}
,
\delta\in\{-1,1\}^{d}
and
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we define
p(x;\nu,\delta)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
to be the vector with entries
(p(x;\nu,\delta))_{j}=\delta_{j}x_{\nu_{j}}
. We say that a function
f\colon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\mapsto\Rbold
is totally rotationally symmetric (TRS) when
f(x)=f(p(x;\nu,\delta))
for all
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{d}
and
\delta\in\{-1,1\}^{d}
.

Total rotational symmetry is natural on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, e.g. the two-point function
G_{z}
as well as the NBW and SRW two-point functions have this symmetry.
We next argue that the NoBLE coefficients have similar symmetries.

[
12
, Assumption 4.1]
.
Let
\iota,\kappa\in\{\pm 1,\pm 2,\dots,\pm d\}
.
The following symmetries hold for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,
z\leq z_{c}
,
N\in\Nbold
and
\iota,\kappa
:

\displaystyle\Xi^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}_{z}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Xi^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}_{z}(-x),\qquad\qquad\qquad\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota}_{z}(x)=\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},-\iota}_{z}(-x),

(3.67)

\displaystyle\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota}_{z}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},-\iota}_{z}(-x),\qquad\qquad\ \Pi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota,\kappa}_{z}(x)=\Pi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},-\iota,-\kappa}_{z}(-x).

(3.68)

For all
N\in\Nbold
, the coefficients

\displaystyle\Xi^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}(x),\qquad\sum_{\iota}\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota}_{z}(x),\qquad\sum_{\iota}\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota}_{z}(x)\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{\iota,\kappa}\Pi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota,\kappa}_{z}(x),

(3.69)

as well as the remainder terms of the split

\displaystyle\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R},z}(x),\quad\sum_{\iota}\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R,I},z}(x),\quad\sum_{\iota}\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R,II},z}(x),\quad\sum_{\iota}\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R,I},z}(x)\quad\sum_{\iota}\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R,II},z}(x),\quad\sum_{\iota,\kappa}\Pi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota,\kappa}_{{\scriptscriptstyle R},z}(x),

(3.70)

are totally rotationally symmetric functions of
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
Further, the dimensions are exchangeable, i.e., for all
\iota,\kappa
,

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota}_{z}(0)=\ \hat{\Psi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\kappa}_{z}(0),\qquad\hat{\Xi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota}_{z}(0)=\ \hat{\Xi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\kappa}_{z}(0),\qquad\sum_{\kappa^{\prime}}\hat{\Pi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota,\kappa^{\prime}}_{z}(0)=\sum_{\iota^{\prime}}\hat{\Pi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota^{\prime},\kappa}_{z}(0).

(3.71)

By the definition of the NoBLE coefficients in Section
3.1
, it is easy to see that (
3.67
), (
3.68
) and (
3.71
) hold.
The TRS stated in (
3.69
), (
3.70
) might be less obvious.
The definition of
\Xi^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}_{z}
does not include constraints on specific directions, so it is not difficult to see that
x\mapsto\Xi^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}_{z}(x)
is TRS for all
N\geq 0
.
The other three NoBLE coefficients are not TRS as their definition includes constraints on one or two specific directions.
For example, the coefficient
\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\kappa}_{z}(x)
includes the constraint that
x-{e}_{\kappa}
is not in the last rib/sausage.
When we sum over
\kappa
, though, the directional constraint is averaged out and thus
\sum_{\kappa}\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\kappa}_{z}(x)
is TRS.
For the same reason, the sums over
\iota
and
\iota,\kappa
in (
3.69
), as well as the stated remainder terms, are TRS.
The arguments given above hold, when the coefficients are well defined, which is definitely the case for all
z<z_{c}
.

[
12
, Assumption 4.1]
states that the symmetry properties also hold for
z=z_{c}
, where it is not even obvious that these objects are well defined.
We verify the left-continuity in
[
12
, Assumption 4.4]
below, from which the symmetries will follow also for
z=z_{c}
.
Further, inspection of the proof in
[
12
]
shows that the symmetries are only used for
z<z_{c}
.
∎

[
12
, Assumption 4.2]
Relation between coefficients.

For all
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,
z\leq z_{c}
,
N\in\Nbold
and
\iota,\kappa\in\{\pm 1,\pm 2,\dots,\pm d\}
, the following bounds hold:

\displaystyle\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\kappa}_{z}(x)\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\mu}_{z}}{\mu_{z}}\Xi^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}_{z}(x),\qquad\qquad\Pi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota,\kappa}_{z}(x)\leq\bar{\mu}_{z}\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota}_{z}(x).

(3.72)

We first compare
\bar{\Xi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}}
and
\bar{\Psi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\kappa}
, in (
3.25
),(
3.27
) and see that they
differ by the additional condition
x-{e}_{\kappa}\not\in S^{\omega}_{|\omega|}
, so that
\bar{\Xi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}}(x)\leq\bar{\Psi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\kappa}(x)
.
Considering the normalisation (
3.33
), we see that
\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}}
was normalised using
g_{z}
, while
\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\kappa}
was normalised using
g^{\iota}_{z}
. As
\bar{\mu}_{z}/\mu_{z}=g_{z}/g^{\iota}_{z}
we conclude that the left inequality in (
3.72
) holds.

Regarding
\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota}
and
\Pi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota,\kappa}
, defined in (
3.26
) and (
3.28
), note that
\Pi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota,\kappa}
contains the extra factor
{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\iota}(\omega)={\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}^{\Pi}_{\iota}(\omega,x,\kappa)
for
N\geq 1
, as well as an additional factor
z
. Further, we do not normalize
\Pi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota,\kappa}
, while
\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota}
is normalized with a factor
1/g_{z}
, creating the stated factor
\bar{\mu}_{z}=zg_{z}
, which proves the bound for
N\geq 1
. To obtain the equation also for
N=0
, we review (
3.21
) and see that
{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\iota}(\omega)
and
{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}^{\Pi}_{\iota}(\omega,x,\kappa)
only differ when
x\neq 0
, which is not possible for LTs, as double connections are not present. For LAs, we see that

\displaystyle zg_{z}\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}(x)-\Pi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota,\kappa}(x)=

\displaystyle\sum_{A\ni 0,x}z^{|A|+1}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle A}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}x\}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{{e}_{\iota}\in A\}}\left(1-{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{x-{e}_{\kappa}\not\in A\}}\right)

\displaystyle-{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{x-{e}_{\kappa}={e}_{\iota}\}}\sum_{A\ni 0,x}z^{|A|+1}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle A}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}x\}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{x\neq 0\}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{x-{e}_{\kappa}\not\in A\}},

(3.73)

and conclude the desired relation for
x\neq{e}_{\iota}+{e}_{\kappa}
, with
\iota\neq-\kappa
. For the remaining case
x={e}_{\iota}+{e}_{\kappa}
we compute

\displaystyle zg_{z}\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}({e}_{\iota}+{e}_{\kappa})-\Pi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota,\kappa}({e}_{\iota}+{e}_{\kappa})=

\displaystyle\sum_{A\ni 0,{e}_{\iota}+{e}_{\kappa}}z^{|A|+1}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle A}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}{e}_{\iota}+{e}_{\kappa}\}}\left({\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{{e}_{\iota}\in A\}}-{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{{e}_{\iota}\not\in A\}}\right).

(3.74)

To verify that this is positive for all
d\geq 30
, we use the following very helpful rearrangement

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{{e}_{\iota}\in A\}}-{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{{e}_{\iota}\not\in A\}}=1-{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{{e}_{\kappa}\not\in A\}}-{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{{e}_{\iota}\not\in A\}},

(3.75)

\displaystyle=1-{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{{e}_{\iota}\not\in A\ni{e}_{\kappa}\}}-{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{{e}_{\kappa}\not\in A\ni{e}_{\iota}\}}-2{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{{e}_{\iota},{e}_{\kappa}\in A\}}={\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{{e}_{\iota},{e}_{\kappa}\in A\}}-{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{{e}_{\iota},{e}_{\kappa}\not\in A\}},

so that

\displaystyle zg_{z}\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}({e}_{\iota}+{e}_{\kappa})-\Pi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota,\kappa}({e}_{\iota}+{e}_{\kappa})=

\displaystyle\sum_{A\ni 0,{e}_{\iota}+{e}_{\kappa}}z^{|A|+1}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle A}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}{e}_{\iota}+{e}_{\kappa}\}}\left({\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{{e}_{\iota},{e}_{\kappa}\in A\}}-{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{{e}_{\iota},{e}_{\kappa}\not\in A\}}\right).

(3.76)

While it is to be expected that this quantity is indeed positive, we could not find a direct injection between the different classes of LAs with the same number of bonds. Thus, we have to resort to a numerical verification of the inequality, using numerical upper and lower bounds.
We add this extra numerical verification in the LA mathematica notebook (see
[
9
, Improvement of Bounds, Technical condition]
), alongside three other numerical conditions that need to be verified for the analysis of
[
12
]
, alike
\hat{G}_{z}(k)\geq 0
for all
k
.

Alternatively, this condition could be dropped, at the expense of having to prove separate bounds for
\Pi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota,\kappa}(x)
, rather than relying on this simple relation to bound
\Pi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota,\kappa}(x)
in terms of
\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}(x)
. We refrain from following this route.
∎

[
12
, Assumption 4.4]
Growth at the critical point.

The functions
z\mapsto\hat{\Xi}_{z}(k),z\mapsto\hat{\Xi}^{\iota}_{z}(k),z\mapsto\hat{\Psi}^{\kappa}_{z}(k),z\mapsto\hat{\Pi}^{\iota,\kappa}_{z}(k)
are continuous for
z\in(0,z_{c})
. Further, let
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\Gamma_{3}\geq 0
be such that
f_{i}(z)\leq\Gamma_{i}
and assume that
[
12
, Assumption 4.3]
holds. Then, the functions stated above are left-continuous in
z_{c}
with a finite limit when
z\nearrow z_{c}
for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Further, for technical reasons, we assume that
z_{c}<1/2
.

The two-point function
G_{z}
is defined as a power series that is clearly continuous in
z
within its radius of convergence
z_{c}
. Since
G^{\iota}_{z}\leq G_{z}
, it has a radius of convergence that is at least
z_{c}
. The coefficients
\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}}_{z},\Xi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}},\iota}_{z},\Psi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}},\iota}_{z}
and
\Pi^{{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}},\iota,\kappa}_{z}
are also power series and can be bounded in terms of many two-point functions. Since
\sum_{x}G_{z}(x)<\infty
for all
z<z_{c}
, also the radii of convergence of these coefficients are at least
z_{c}
. [We believe that
G^{\iota}_{z}
and the NoBLE coefficients have the same radii of convergence as
G_{z}
, but that is irrelevant here.]
Assuming that the bootstrap functions are uniformly bounded, all stated functions are uniformly bounded as well, which implies that they are left-continuous at
z_{c}
by Abel’s theorem.

To prove that
z_{c}<1/2
consider a simple random walk that only takes steps in the positive direction. After
n
steps there are
d^{n}
possible trajectories, each being also a LT, so that
t_{n}(0)\geq d^{n}
.
As
z_{c}
is defined as the radius of convergence of
\chi
(see (
1.3
)), and

\displaystyle\chi(1/d)

\displaystyle=\hat{\bar{G}}_{1/d}(0)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{x}t_{n}(x)d^{-n}\geq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}t_{n}(0)d^{-n}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}d^{n}d^{-n}=\infty,

we know that
z_{c}\leq 1/d<1/2
.

4
An overview to the NoBLE diagrammatic bounds

In this section we discuss how we bound the NoBLE coefficients. We explain the concepts that we use to obtain sharp bound on these diagrams, each of which forms a numerical improvement on the bounds on classical lace-expansion coefficients. Then, we follow that up with the technical definitions of these concepts. We end this section with a discussion of how we bound double connections for LAs, as these are quite central in our bounds for LAs.

4.1
Basic bounds and their improvements

We explain here how we obtain sharp bounds on

\displaystyle\bar{\Xi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}}_{z}(x)=

\displaystyle\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{W}(x)}z^{|\omega|}Z[0,|\omega|]J^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}[0,|\omega|]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{W}(x)}z^{|\omega|}\prod_{i=0}^{|\omega|}z^{|S^{\omega}_{i}|}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{S^{\omega}_{s}\text{ and }S^{\omega}_{t}\text{ are only intersecting for $s=0$ and $t=|\omega|$}\}},

(4.1)

by using four ideas, one at a time. We restrict here to LTs, as these give rise to the simplest diagrams, which already display all the main ingredients to our bounds.

We compare the improvement that we obtain in our bounds by providing numerical bounds for the initial point
z=z_{I}
resulting from the numerical analysis performed in our Mathematica notebooks. Our proof is based upon the fact that the values at
z_{I}
and at
z_{c}
are actually quite close. Thus, even though
z_{c}>z_{I}
,
such bounds should give us a clear idea of how large such bounds are, and what the effect of the improvements is.

We recall (
3.54
) and combine it with the bound
g^{\iota}_{z_{I}}\leq{\mathrm{e}}
, see (
A.6
), to conclude, for
z_{I}=(2d-1)^{-1}{\mathrm{e}}^{-1}
,

\displaystyle\bar{G}_{z_{I}}(x)\leq g_{z_{I}}^{\iota}B_{1/(2d-1)}(x)={\mathrm{e}}B(x),

(4.2)

where
{\mathrm{e}}
is the Euler number and
B(x)
critical two-point function of the NBW, see (
2.5
). This bound is independent of the lace expansion and we can compute its value numerically. While this bound does not hold at the critical point
z_{c}
, its value is good enough to compare the bounds discussed in this section. All stated numerical results are computed for dimension
d=18
, and are aimed to convey the numerical improvements in the bounds.

Plain-vanilla bound.

We focus on
\bar{\Xi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}}_{z}
and start with the simplest possible approach, which relies on the bounds as they have been stated up to now in the literature.
The coefficient
\bar{\Xi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}}_{z}
involves three points:
0
,
x
and a point where
S^{\omega}_{0}
and
S^{\omega}_{|\omega|}
intersect, which we denote by
w
.
Bounding the connections between each of the points by individual independent LTs we obtain

\displaystyle\bar{\Xi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}}_{z}(x)\leq

\displaystyle\bar{G}_{z}(x)\sum_{w}\bar{G}_{z}(w-x)\bar{G}_{z}(w),

(4.3)

which, combined with (
4.2
), implies, for
z\leq z_{I}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{x}\bar{\Xi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}}_{z_{I}}(x)\leq

\displaystyle(\bar{G}_{z_{I}})^{\star 3}(0)\leq{\mathrm{e}}^{3}B^{\star 3}(0)=22.322\dots

(4.4)

This is of course a very bad bound that does not allow us to successfully apply the lace-expansion method, so we improve it using the four ideas explained below.

First improvement: extracting trivial lines.

A simple way to improve this bound is to consider four cases depending on which lines are trivial:
x=0
, then
w=x\neq 0
and
w=0\neq x
and the remaining cases.
In the case
x=0
, we extract the contribution of the first rib
g_{z}^{\iota}
and the first bond of the backbone
with all its possible directions
2dz
, and bound the remaining rib-walk, that goes back to the origin, by
\bar{G}_{z}({e}_{1})
.
Thus, the case
x=0
is bounded by
2dzg_{z}^{\iota}(D\star\bar{G}_{z})(0)
.

For
w=x\neq 0
and
w=0\neq x
,
2
non-trivial two-point functions are required. For
w\not\in\{0,x\}
, instead, all three connections are non-trivial.
We bound the non-trivial connections by
\bar{G}_{z_{I}}(x)\leq{\mathrm{e}}B(x)\leq\tfrac{2d{\mathrm{e}}}{2d-1}(D\star B)(x)
for
x\neq 0
and conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{x}\bar{\Xi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}}_{z_{I}}(x)\leq

\displaystyle{\mathrm{e}}\Big(\frac{2d}{2d-1}\Big)^{2}(D^{\star 2}\star B)(0)+2{\mathrm{e}}^{2}\Big(\frac{2d}{2d-1}\Big)^{2}(D^{\star 2}\star B^{\star 2})(0)

\displaystyle+{\mathrm{e}}^{3}\Big(\frac{2d}{2d-1}\Big)^{3}(D^{\star 3}\star B^{\star 3})(0)=0.85541\ldots

(4.5)

This is an enormous improvement compared to (
4.4
). The simple idea of extracting trivial lines, by splitting between different cases, is a basic technique that is used for all coefficients in Section
6
.

Second improvement: improved counting of one-point functions by rib allocation.

We observe that we extract a single one-point function at every line in an intersection point. This gives rise to the three factors of
{\mathrm{e}}
in (
4.5
).
However, when the different lines come from the
same
rib, there in fact is only
one
one-point function, so that we are overcounting one one-point function (leading to a factor
{\mathrm{e}}
)
every time we split a rib. Thus, for the diagram in (
4.4
), we would need only
one
factor
{\mathrm{e}}
instead of three.
Let us explain one way to obtain a bound without overcounting.

In (
4.3
), the terms
\bar{G}_{z}(w)
and
\bar{G}_{z}(w-x)
bound the ribs
S^{\omega}_{0}
and
S^{\omega}_{|\omega|}
, which, being ribs, are just LTs.
The indicator
J^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}[0,|\omega|]
in (
4.1
) guarantees that only those
\omega
contribute for which all in-between ribs do not intersect, so that the rib-walk between

\overline{b}_{1}^{\omega}=u
and
\overline{b}^{\omega}_{|\omega|}=v
also describes a LT and can be bounded by
\bar{G}_{z}(v-u)
.
Extracting the case that
|\omega|=1
, for which
\overline{b}_{1}^{\omega}=\overline{b}^{\omega}_{|\omega|}
,
so that there are no in-between ribs, we obtain

\displaystyle\bar{\Xi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}}_{z}(x)\leq

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{x=0\}}2dzg_{z}^{\iota}(D\star\bar{G}_{z})(0)

(4.6)

\displaystyle+{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{x\neq 0\}}\sum_{w}\bar{G}_{z}(w)\bar{G}_{z}(w-x)\Big({\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{|x|=1\}}z+z^{2}\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u,v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}{{|u|=|x-v|=1}}}\bar{G}_{z}(v-u)\Big),

where
z
and
z^{2}
correspond to the weight of the first and last bond of the original backbone.
Considering the four cases for
0,x,w
, used in the first improvement, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{x}\bar{\Xi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}}_{z_{I}}(x)\leq

\displaystyle 3{\mathrm{e}}\Big(\frac{2d}{2d-1}\Big)^{2}(D^{\star 2}\star B)(0)+{\mathrm{e}}\Big(\frac{2d}{2d-1}\Big)^{3}(D^{\star 3}\star B^{\star 2})(0)

\displaystyle+2{\mathrm{e}}\Big(\frac{2d}{2d-1}\Big)^{3}(D^{\star 3}\star B^{\star 2})(0)+{\mathrm{e}}\Big(\frac{2d}{2d-1}\Big)^{4}(D^{\star 3}\star B^{\star 3})(0)=0.5725\dots

(4.7)

The improvement of (
4.5
) to (
4.7
) is mostly realized by the proper handling
of the weights of the one-point function
g^{\iota}_{z}\approx{\mathrm{e}}
at the origin and at
x
, which are bounded twice in (
4.5
).

Remark 4.1

(Rib-weight allocation)
.

In general, the diagram of
\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}}_{z}
has
4N-1
connections for LTs. Allocating the one-point functions properly will save us approximately a factor
{\mathrm{e}}^{4N-2}
for all
N
. This allocation of rib weights is a central technical problem, especially as we require various different allocations for the weights
when we bound the coefficient with spatial weight
|x|^{2}
. Using splitting arguments of such spatial terms, that are standard in lace-expansion analyses, we split the weight along a path connecting
0
and
x
,
e.g. using
|x|^{2}\leq N\sum_{i=1}^{N}|x_{i}|^{2}
, where the
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{N}
are the displacements along the bottom lines of the diagram,
so that
x=\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}
. For each of these partial weights
|x_{i}|^{2}
, we want to bound the corresponding connection
by
\bar{G}_{z}
, as
f_{3}
only provides us with a bound on
|x_{i}|^{2}\bar{G}_{z}(x_{i})
.
This imposes severe restrictions on our rib-weight allocation, and we need to be really careful in such arguments.
We discuss this in more detail in Section
4.2
, see also Remark
4.5
.

Third improvement: using the non-backtracking nature of the diagram.

Now, we use the non-backtracking property of the rib-walk for the first time. This property implies that any loop needs at least
4
bonds,
as direct reversals are prohibited and loops on the lattice require an even number of bonds.
For example the self-loop of the rib-walk back to
x=0
will take at least
4
steps and can be bounded by

\displaystyle(2d{z_{I}}g_{z_{I}}^{\iota})^{3}(D^{\star 3}\star\bar{G}_{z_{I}})(0)\leq\left(\frac{2d}{2d-1}\right)^{3}\frac{2d{\mathrm{e}}}{2d-1}(D^{\star 4}\star B)(0).

(4.8)

Further, we can omit the case
w\in\{0,x\}
for the case that
b_{1}^{\omega}=(0,x)
, see property (v) of Definition
3.2
.
Going through
8
different combinations of how the
4
steps can be distributed, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{x}\bar{\Xi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}}_{z_{I}}(x)\leq

\displaystyle 4{\mathrm{e}}\big(\frac{2d}{2d-1}\big)^{4}(D^{\star 4}\star B)(0)+3{\mathrm{e}}\big(\frac{2d}{2d-1}\big)^{4}(D^{\star 4}\star B^{\star 2})(0)

\displaystyle+{\mathrm{e}}\big(\frac{2d}{2d-1}\big)^{4}(D^{\star 3}\star B^{\star 3})(0)=0.0755\dots

(4.9)

The improvement from (
4.7
) to (
4.9
) demonstrate the power of the NoBLE and its non-backtracking property. To use this property for the other diagrams, we will split depending on the lengths of lines shared by two loops. This splitting into a total of five cases of lengths of shared lines will create
matrix-based bounds.
This will become apparent in Section
5.1
.

Fourth improvement: using repulsiveness in diagrams.

We can actually improve this bound even further. For this we have to understand that the paths connecting
0
,
x
and
w
do not intersect, so that the loop is actually a self-avoiding polygon. We call such a polygon a
repulsive diagram
. For this to work, we have to choose the point
w_{1}
as a
first intersection point
and bound it by a
repulsive
diagram. Both terms are defined within the next two sections. Using these repulsive diagrams, we improve the bound (
4.9
) to

\displaystyle\sum_{x}\bar{\Xi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}}_{z_{I}}(x)\leq

\displaystyle 0.02562\dots

(4.10)

This is explained in more detail in Section
4.3
.

Summary.

By the above four improvements combined, we have now improved our estimate on this simple diagram
\sum_{x}\bar{\Xi}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}}_{z_{I}}(x)
by a factor that is close to 1000. Since we need all the numerical precision that we can get our hands upon, so as to be able to apply our methods in the lowest possible dimensions, such estimates are crucial to the success of our method.

Extension to lattice animals.

Lattice animals (LAs) allow for double connections. Despite this additional difficulty, for
\underline{b}_{0}=0
and
\overline{b}_{|\omega|}=x
, we can use the same bounds (
4.3
)-(
4.10
) based on exactly the same arguments. For
\underline{b}_{0}\neq 0
we need to bound the sausage, which connects the points
0,\underline{b}_{0}
, as well as the first intersection point
w_{1}
. We explain this in Section
4.2
, where we also define the required notions. In Section
4.4
, we discuss how we use a symmetry argument to improve the bound for the important case
w_{1}\in\{0,\underline{b}_{0}\}
of a double connection.

4.2
Allocation of one-point functions

In the preceding section, we disassembled the rib-walk into up to three pieces: the initial rib, the final rib and the walk in between, and have argued that rib-weight allocation is a crucial ingredient to the success of our method. In the following, we also need to split individual ribs/sausages. Below we give a rigorous description of the split of a rib/LT and explain how we ensure that we do not overcount one-point functions by an optimal rib-weight allocation.

After this, we explain the allocation of one-point functions for the more involved diagrams. In the process we define the concept of
first intersection point
of two sausages, that will allow use to choose bond-disjoint paths connecting the corner points of the loops. In the next section we use this choice of bond-disjoint paths to define
repulsive diagrams
.

Splitting the weight of a rib: planted animals/trees.

Our aim is to bound the contribution
z^{|T|}
of LTs that contain the vertices
0,x,v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
efficiently. We identify the last vertex
u
that the path
0\leftrightarrow x
and the path
0\leftrightarrow v
have in common, split the tree into three trees and ignore the avoidance constraints between them to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{T}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{0,x,v\in T\}}z^{|T|}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}}\sum_{u\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{0,u\in T_{1}\}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{x,u\in T_{2}\}}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{u,v\in T_{3}\}}z^{|T_{1}|+|T_{2}|+|T_{3}|}

\displaystyle=\sum_{u}\bar{G}_{z}(u)\bar{G}_{z}(x-u)\bar{G}_{z}(v-u),

(4.11)

which corresponds to the plain-vanilla bound of the last section. Thus, it also counts
three
ribs at
u
, whereas there really only is one.

To avoid this, we define an adapted version of the two-point function that only counts the trivial first rib/sausage
A^{\omega}_{0}=\varnothing
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\tilde{G}_{z}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{W}(x)}z^{|\omega|}Z[0,|\omega|]K[0,|\omega|]{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{A^{\omega}_{0}=\varnothing\}}.

(4.12)

Using this two-point function, we obtain the bound

\displaystyle\sum_{T}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{0,x,v\in T\}}z^{|T|}\leq\sum_{u}\bar{G}_{z}(u)\tilde{G}_{z}(x-u)\tilde{G}_{z}(v-u),

(4.13)

which is approximately
{\mathrm{e}}^{2}
smaller than (
4.11
).

Planted animals.

For LTs, the point
u
at which we split the tree is unique, which is not the case for LA. We define the concepts of
backbone
,
connecting planted animal
and

first point of intersection
, to provide a rigorous way to split the sausage for LAs. We only give the definition for LAs and sausage-walks, as each LT is also a LA and each rib-walk is also a sausage-walk.

Before defining these notions, we discuss the
lexicographic order
on paths, which is a way to identify paths uniquely. We say that
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
has a lower lexicographic order than
y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, if there exists an
i\in\{1,2,\dots,d\}
with
x_{i}<y_{i}
and
x_{j}=y_{j}
for
j=1,2,\dots,i-1
. A bond
b
is defined as a tuple of two vertices
b=(\underline{b},\overline{b})
, so that we can view
b
also to be a vector in
\Zbold^{2d}
and use the same order relations as for vertices. A path
(b_{1},b_{2},\dots,b_{n})
has a lower lexicographic order than the path
(t_{1},t_{2},\dots,t_{m})
, if either there exists a
i\in\{1,2,\dots,\min(n,m)\}
, such that
b_{i}
has a lower order than
t_{i}
and
b_{j}=t_{j}
for
j=1,2,\dots,i-1
or if
n<m
and
b_{i}=t_{i}
for
i=1,2,\dots,n
.

Definition 4.2

(Backbone)
.

Let
A
be a lattice animal containing
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, with
x\neq y
.
A path from
x
to
y
in
A
is a sequence of bonds
(b_{i})_{i=1,\dots,N}
such that
b_{i}\in A,\overline{b}_{i}\neq\overline{b}_{j}
for all
i\neq j
, and
\underline{b}_{1}=x,\overline{b}_{i}=\underline{b}_{i+1},\overline{b}_{N}=y
for
i=1,\dots,N-1
.
We define
b^{A}(x,y)
to be the path from to
x
to
y
in
A
with the lowest lexicographic order.
For
x=y\in A
, we define
b^{A}(x,y)=\varnothing
.

Definition 4.3

(Connecting planted animal)
.

Let
A
be a lattice animal containing
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, with
x\neq y
, and let
p
be a path from
x
to
y
in
A
.
Let
S
be the subset of
A\setminus\{p\}
of all bonds for which at least one vertex of the bond is connected to
x
via bonds in
A\setminus\{p\}
.
We call
B^{A}(x,y;p)=A\setminus S
the
connecting planted animal
from
x
to
y
along
p
.

Unless stated otherwise, we consider the path
p
to be the backbone
p=b^{A}(x,y)
and omit
p
from the notation.
For a sausage-/rib-walk
\omega
, we define
B^{\omega}_{i}(x,y)=B^{A^{\omega}_{i}}(x,y)
.

x
y
B^{A}(x,y)
x
y
B^{A}(x,y)

Figure 7
:
Two examples of a connecting planted animal.

We next define the notion of a
first intersection point.
We do this in a more general setting, encoded by a lace
L
, where some of the LAs are forced to intersect each other, while others are not. This will make sure that we can use this definition for all NoBLE lace-expansion coefficients:

Definition 4.4

(First intersection point)
.

For a sausage-walk
\omega
, a lace
L=\{s_{1}t_{1},s_{2}t_{2},

\dots,

s_{M}t_{M}\}
and
i\leq M
, we define
W_{i}(\omega)
to be the set of vertices
w
that are contained in both
A^{\omega}_{s_{i}}
and
A^{\omega}_{t_{i}}
, such that
b^{A^{\omega}_{s_{i}}}(\overline{b}^{\omega}_{s_{i}},w)
and
A^{\omega}_{t_{i}}(\overline{b}^{\omega}_{t_{i}},w)
only intersect at
w
. If
W_{i}(\omega)
is non-empty, then we define
w_{i}(\omega)
to be the smallest in the set
W_{i}(\omega)
.
We call
w_{i}(\omega)
the
first intersection point
of
A^{\omega}_{s_{i}}
and
A^{\omega}_{t_{i}}
.

Note that if the end of the
s_{i}
th pivotal bond
\overline{b}^{\omega}_{s_{i}}
is in
S^{\omega}_{t_{i}}
, then
W_{i}(\omega)=\{\overline{b}^{\omega}_{s_{i}}\}
.
We illustrate the concept of a first intersection point in Figure
8
.

B_{t_{N}}^{\omega}
\overline{b}^{\omega}_{t_{N}}
B_{s_{N}}^{\omega}
\overline{b}^{\omega}_{s_{N}}

B_{s_{N}}^{\omega}

w_{a}

w_{c}

Figure 8
:

Two examples of the set of first intersection points
W_{N}(\omega)=\{w_{a},w_{b},w_{c}\}
.
In the picture the
s_{N}
th and
t_{N}
th sausage of the sausage-walk
\omega
intersect.
We choose the point
w_{N}(\omega)
to be the unique smallest representative of
W_{N}(\omega)
in the lexicographic order.

Non-trivial sausages.

If we wish to extend the analysis to LAs, then we need to deal with non-trivial sausages. A lattice animal
A
is called a
non-trivial sausage
if it contains three vertices
0,x,w
(with
x\neq 0
), such that
0
and
x
are doubly connected in
A
, as shown in the first image of Figure
9
. We now show how we bound these sausages.
In Section
4.4
, we explain how we obtain improved bounds for the special case
w\in\{0,x\}
, which is a major contribution for LAs.

We define the point
u
to be a point that every path from
0
to
w
and
x
to
w
shares. By construction, the points
0,u,x
are pairwise doubly connected, so that there exist four bond-disjoint paths:
p_{1}(u,w),p_{2}(u,x),p_{3}(0,u)
and
p_{4}(0,x)
connecting the indicated points in
A
. We bound the contribution of such LAs by

\displaystyle\sum_{A\colon 0,x,w\in A}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle A}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}x\}}z^{|A|}\leq

\displaystyle 2dz\sum_{u}\tilde{G}_{z}(u-w)(D\star\tilde{G}_{z})(u-x)\tilde{G}_{z}(u)\bar{G}_{z}(x).

(4.14)

We obtain this bound by decomposing each LA
A
into four parts as shown in Figure
9
. Using the pictured decomposition, we know that
A_{4}
is an animal containing
x
, which we bound by
\bar{G}_{z}(x)
. Further,
A_{1}
and
A_{3}
, respectively, only include one bond that contains
u
and
0
, respectively.
Thus, we bound them using the modified two-point function
\tilde{G}_{z}
. The animal
A_{2}
is the interesting case as
u
and
x
are only connected by one bond each. If
u\neq x
, then we bound this contribution by the step involved by
2dzD(\cdot)
and the remainder by
\tilde{G}_{z}
, creating the bound
2dz(D\star\tilde{G}_{z})(x-u)
.
If
u=x
then
A_{2}=\varnothing
and
A_{3}
only has one bond at the origin and one at
x
, so we bound
A_{3}
by
2dz(D\star\tilde{G}_{z})(x)
.

x
u

A

containing

and the last common point

on the paths from

and

.

Let

.

Figure 9
:
The way that we split a LA to create the bound (
4.14
).

There are multiple ways to split such animals, and depending on the situation we might want to use a different combination of where to attribute the rib weights. To give an example, we can create animals as discussed above in which each line is bounded by
\tilde{G}_{z}
. For this, we modify the split shown in Figure
9
as follows:

i.)

Let
R_{3}=B^{R_{2}}(x,0;p_{4}(x,0))
, and
R_{4}=R_{2}\setminus R_{3}
.

ii.)

Let
\bar{A}_{3}=B^{R_{2}}(0,u;p_{3}(0,u))
and
\bar{A}_{4}=R_{3}\setminus\bar{A}_{3}
.

iii.)

Let
\bar{A}_{2}=R_{4}\cup A_{2}
.

The split into
A_{1},\bar{A}_{2},\bar{A}_{3}
and
\bar{A}_{4}
is very much alike the one shown in Figure
9
, the only difference
being that every bond incident to
x
that is not part of the path
p_{4}(x,0)
is now part of
\bar{A}_{2}
instead of
\bar{A}_{4}
.
This creates the bound

\displaystyle\sum_{A\colon 0,x,w\in A}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle A}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}x\}}z^{|A|}\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{u}\tilde{G}_{z}(u-w)\tilde{G}_{z}(u-x)\tilde{G}_{z}(u)\tilde{G}_{z}(x).

(4.15)

Which of the different bounds is chosen depends on the precise nature of the bound that we are deriving. In fact, optimizing over such decisions is numerically an important ingredient of our method.

Bound on the sausage-walk in
\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}}_{z}(x)
.

Here we continue our bound on
\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}}_{z}(x)
started in Section
4.1
, now specializing to LAs with a non-trivial first sausage.
We have bounded the case
\underline{b}_{1}=0
by splitting the sausage-walk into three parts:

(a)

The first sausage
S^{\omega}_{0}
that connects
0
and
w_{1}(\omega)
, bounded by
\bar{G}_{z}(w)
;

(b)

The last sausage
S^{\omega}_{|\omega|}
that connects
x
and
w_{1}(\omega)
, bounded by
\bar{G}_{z}(w-x)
;

(c)

The remaining sausage-walk without the first and last sausage, bounded by
2dz(D\star\tilde{G}_{z})(x)
.

For
\underline{b}_{1}\neq 0
, which is not possible for LTs, we need to split the first sausage
S^{\omega}_{0}
.
The first sausage contains
0,\underline{b}_{1}
and
w_{1}(\omega)
and doubly connects
0
and
\underline{b}_{1}
.
Thus,
S^{\omega}_{0}
is a non-trivial sausage as discussed in the previous paragraph.
Combining these bounds we arrive at

\displaystyle\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}}_{z}(x)\leq

\displaystyle 2dz\sum_{w}\bar{G}_{z}(w)\bar{G}_{z}(w-x)(D\star\tilde{G}_{z})(x)

(4.16)

\displaystyle+2dz\sum_{w,\underline{b},u}\bar{G}_{z}(w-x)(D\star\tilde{G}_{z})(x-\underline{b})\tilde{G}_{z}(u-w)\tilde{G}_{z}(u-x)\tilde{G}_{z}(u)\tilde{G}_{z}(\underline{b}),

where the second term is absent for LTs.

\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 2})}}_{z}

\displaystyle I_{S1}=

\displaystyle I_{S2}=

\displaystyle B^{R_{2}}_{0}(0,u),\quad I_{S3}=R_{3}\setminus I_{S2},\quad\text{and}\quad I_{S4}=R_{4}\cup A_{2}.

The trimmed sausage-walk then create

By this construction, all ten paths connecting the labeled points are bond-disjoint and each piece is a LA.

All pieces with sub-index
S
are planted animals and can be bounded by
\tilde{G}_{z}
.
For the backbone lines
II_{B},III_{B},IV_{B}
, we have to decide where to bound the sausages at the labeled points.
We can bound two of the pieces using
\tilde{G}_{z}
, taking into account only the weight of either the first or final sausage.
The third piece has to be bounded using
\bar{G}_{z}
, which bounds the sausages on both ends.
Thus, we bound only one connection by
\bar{G}_{z}
, rather than all four, which is a major improvement.
Using a slightly different split of the sausage-walk, we are actually free to choose any line to be bounded by
\bar{G}_{z}
.
This will be useful when dealing with weighted diagrams, we discuss this in detail in Section
6.2
.

For the case

The rest is spit in the same way as described for the case

.
