: For a sausage-walk
\omega
and a lace
L=\{0t_{1},s_{2}|\omega|\}
to which
\omega
contributes we define
w_{1}=w_{1}(\omega),\ w_{2}=w_{2}(\omega)
and
y=\overline{b}^{\omega}_{s_{2}}
. If
s_{2}<t_{1}
, then we define
u=\omega_{s_{1}}
and
l=\max\{d_{\omega}(u,y),2\}
. For
s_{2}=t_{1}
we identify the last point
u
that the paths
b^{S^{\omega}_{s_{2}}}(\underline{b}^{\omega}_{s_{2}+1},v)
and
b^{S^{\omega}_{s_{2}}}(\underline{b}^{\omega}_{s_{2}+1},w)
have in common and define
l=-\max\{d_{A^{\omega}_{s_{2}}}(\underline{b}^{\omega}_{s_{2}+1},u),2\}
.

By definition of a sausage-walk (Definition
3.3
), using the non-backtracking condition, and the selection of the first intersection points
w_{1},w_{2}
, we know that each square is repulsive and consists of at least four bonds. Especially, if
t_{1}=1
, so that
S^{\omega}_{0}
and
S^{\omega}_{1}
intersect, then the non-backtracking condition states that this intersection cannot occur at the backbone bond, i.e.,
w_{1}\not\in b_{1}^{\omega}
. For the same reason we know that
w_{2}\not\in b_{|\omega|}^{\omega}
in the cases of
|\omega|-s_{2}=1
and
\overline{b}_{|\omega|}^{\omega}=x
.
This last observations look like a minute special case, but it is numerically a major contribution.

To obtain (
6.31
), we let the line
u,y
contribute to the left square.
When we add the weight
|x-y|^{2}
, we obtain (
6.32
).
When considering the weight
|y|^{2}
, we choose the split of the diagram such that
the line
u,y
contributes to the right square instead. This creates the bound (
6.33
).
To prove (
6.31
)-(
6.33
), we have to go through all cases for
l
and
check that all possible sausage-walks are captured by the bounds on the right-hand sides. These tedious arguments
follow the same steps as in the proof of the bounds on the coefficients for
N=1
in the previous section, and we omit the details.
The bound on
\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 2})},\iota}_{z}
is obtained in the same way, using the initial piece
P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})},\iota,m}_{z}
, sketched in Section
5.1
.

Proof for
N\geq 3
.

The bounds for
N\geq 3
are proven following the same procedure as described for
N=2
. For each sausage-walk
\omega
and lace
L
,
we identify the points at which we split
\omega
, these are
\underline{b}_{s_{i}}
,
\underline{b}_{t_{i}}
, as well as the first intersection points
w_{i}(\omega)
.
Whenever
s_{i}=t_{i-1}
, we also identify the point at which we split the sausage
S_{s_{i}}^{\omega}
, see Figures
10
and
11
.
Then, we define the lengths of the shared lines of neighboring loops
l_{i}
accordingly.
For a bound on the weighted diagrams, we then split the weight using (
6.24
), leading to a sum of weighted diagrams as in (
6.26
), and for each of these summands, we derive a bound as in (
6.32
)-(
6.34
) depending on which line is weighted.

We now give some insight into how these bounds can be proven. As all these arguments do not offer any further
insight, we omit their formal proof and refer the interested reader to
[
10
, Section 4.3.5]
, where more details are given.
It involves defining skeletons that encode (i) the length of the pieces of shared lines of neighboring loops; (ii) the location of the
backbone; and (iii) the partial displacements
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{N}
.
Then, we classify all pairs or rib-/sausage-walks and laces
(\omega,L)
uniquely according to their skeleton and
prove the bound using induction on
N
. The proof strategy uses ideas already presented above and techniques described
in the standard reference
[
42
]
.

Bound on
\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota}_{z}
.

The coefficients
\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}}_{z}
and
\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota}_{z}
differ only by the first sausage, as informally explained in the foundation block
P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})},m}
and
P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})},\iota,m}
.
Thus, also the bounds differ only in the first block. The block
P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})},\iota,m}
can have very many forms and the bounds on its weighted versions
\Delta^{\iota,{\scriptscriptstyle I},m}
and

\Delta^{\iota,{\scriptscriptstyle II},m}
have to be done with care, as they are numerically important.

We provide a complete definition of the bounding diagrams
P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})},\iota,m}
,
\Delta^{\iota,{\scriptscriptstyle I},m}
and

\Delta^{\iota,{\scriptscriptstyle II},m}
that list all possible cases, and rigorously derive their corresponding bounds in
Appendices
C.2.2
-
C.4.2
.
∎

Decomposition using matrices.

In the recursive definitions of
P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},m}
,
P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota,m}
,
Q^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},m}
, in
(
6.27
)-(
6.30
), as well as in the bounds of Lemma
6.2
, we sum over the lengths of lines that are shared by two loops. We do this as it allows us to make use of the fact that any loop consists of at least four bonds.
We convert this sum over line lengths into a product of matrices to obtain the bounds stated in Proposition
5.4
.
Here we explain how we do this. We start at the definition of
P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},m}
in (
6.27
) and obtain

\displaystyle P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},m}_{z}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},m}_{z}(x,y)=\sum_{l=-2}^{2}\sum_{u,v,x,y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N-1})},l}_{z}(u,u+v)A^{l,m}(u,u+v,x,y)

(6.38)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=-2}^{2}\sum_{u,v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N-1})},l}(u,v)\sup_{v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{x,y}A^{l,m}(0,v,x,y)=\sum_{l}P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N-1})},m}_{z}({\bf A})_{l,m},

where we recall (
5.4
). Using this recursion, we can prove by induction and using (
5.1
), that

\displaystyle P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},m}_{z}\leq(\vec{P}^{T}{\bf A}^{N-1})_{m}.

Combining this with (
6.31
),
we obtain the bound on
\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}}_{z}
as stated in (
5.36
) by bounding

\displaystyle\sum_{x}\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}}_{z}(x)\leq

\displaystyle\frac{g^{\iota}_{z}}{g_{z}}\sum_{u,v,l,x}P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N-1})},l}_{z}(u,v)A^{l,0}(u,v,x,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{g^{\iota}_{z}}{g_{z}}\sum_{u,v,l,x}P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N-1})},l}_{z}(u,u+v)A^{l,0}(0,v,x,x)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{g^{\iota}_{z}}{g_{z}}\sum_{l}\left(\sum_{u,v}P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N-1})},l}_{z}(u,u+v)\right)\left(\sup_{v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{x}A^{l,0}(0,v,x,x)\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:defOfBoundingElement2}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\frac{g^{\iota}_{z}}{g_{z}}\sum_{l}(\vec{P}^{T}{\bf A}^{N-2})_{l}(\vec{E}^{\rm{closed}})_{l}=\frac{g^{\iota}_{z}}{g_{z}}\vec{P}^{T}{\bf A}^{N-2}{\vec{E}}^{\rm{open}}.

(6.39)

The bound on
\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota}_{z}
is obtained in the same way, where
P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},l}_{z}
is replaced with
P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota,l}_{z}
.

Using translation invariance for
{Q}_{z}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm M})},m}
, defined in (
6.29
)–(
6.30
), we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{v,w\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}{Q}_{z}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm M})},m}(w,y;x)=

\displaystyle\sum_{w,y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}Q_{z}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm M})},m}(w,y;0)=\sum_{y,x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}Q_{z}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm M})},m}(0,y;x)

(6.40)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:PnConstructLT-Qm}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{l=-2}^{2}\sum_{u,v,x,y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}Q_{z}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm M-1})},l}(0,v;x)A^{l,m}(u,u+v,0,y)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{l=-2}^{2}\left(\sum_{x,v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}Q^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm M-1})},l}(0,v;x)\right)\left(\sup_{v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{u,y}A^{l,m}(u,u+v,0,y)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle({\bf A}^{M-1}\vec{E}^{\rm{closed}})_{m}.

Combining (
6.38
), (
6.40
) with (
6.37
), we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|x|^{2}\Xi_{z}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}}(x)\leq

\displaystyle N\sum_{l}\Big(\sup_{v}\sum_{x_{1}}\Delta^{{\rm start},l}(x_{1},v)\Big)({\bf A}^{N-2}\vec{E}^{\rm{closed}})_{l}

\displaystyle+N\sum_{M=1}^{N-2}(\vec{P}^{T}{\bf A}^{M-2})_{m}\Big(\sup_{v,y}\sum_{w}C^{m,l}(0,v,w,w+y)\Big)({\bf A}^{N-M-2}\vec{E}^{\rm{closed}})_{l}

\displaystyle+N\sum_{l}(\vec{P}^{T}{\bf A}^{N-2})_{l}\Big(\sup_{v}\sum_{x_{N}}\Delta^{{\rm end},l}(0,v,x_{N},x_{N})\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:defOfBoundingElement1},\eqref{e:defOfBoundingElement2}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle N(\vec{\Delta}^{\rm{start}})^{T}{\bf A}^{N-2}{\vec{E}^{\rm{closed}}}+N\sum_{M=1}^{N-2}\vec{P}^{T}{\bf A}^{M-2}{\bf C}{\bf A}^{N-M-2}\vec{E}^{\rm{closed}}

\displaystyle+N\vec{P}^{T}{\bf A}^{N-2}\vec{\Delta}^{{\rm end}},

(6.41)

which proves (
5.37
). The bound on
\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota}
are proven in the same way, using other initial diagrams.

Let us briefly mention a numerical aspect here. For
|m|=1
, we only count
u,v
that are neighbors.
By spatial symmetry,

\displaystyle\sup_{v}\sum_{u}P_{z}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},m}(u,u+v)=\sum_{u}P_{z}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},m}(u,u+{e}_{1})=\frac{1}{2d}\sum_{u,w}P_{z}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},m}(u,u+w).

(6.42)

This means that the step of taking the supremum over
v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, while disassembling
P_{z}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},m}
and
{Q}_{z}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm M})},m}
in (
6.38
) and (
6.40
), actually holds with an equality for
|m|\leq 1
. We also apply this method to
P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota,m}_{z}
, when we simply sum over
\iota
, to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{u,\iota}P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota,m}_{z}(u,u+{e}_{\kappa})=\sum_{u,\iota}P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota,m}_{z}(u,u+{e}_{1}).

(6.43)

6.4
Bounds on differences: Proof of Lemma
5.5

Now we turn to the bounds stated in Lemma
5.5
.
By definition
\Xi^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})}_{\alpha,z}(0)=0
, see (
3.36
).
Further, we recall that, now by (
3.42
),

\displaystyle\Xi^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}_{\alpha,z}(x)=\frac{1}{g_{z}}\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{W}(x)}z^{|\omega|}Z[0,|\omega|]J^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}}[0,|\omega|]{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}_{\{\underline{b}^{\omega}_{0}=0\}}.

(6.44)

For
x=0
, the sausage-walk needs to return to the origin.
Note that the indicator
J^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}}[0,|\omega|]
enforces an intersection between the zeroth and the last sausage, which in fact is implied also by the fact that
\omega\in\mathcal{W}(0)
and
\underline{b}^{\omega}_{0}=0
. However,
J^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}}[0,|\omega|]
also forces the other sausages to avoid each other. Thus, the sausage-walk, without the initial sausage and
b^{\omega}_{0}
, form a lattice animal and can be bounded by
\bar{G}_{3,z}(\overline{b}^{\omega}_{0})
. We bound the first sausage by the factor
zg^{\iota}_{z}
, resulting in the bound

\displaystyle 0\leq\Xi^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}_{\alpha,z}(0)\leq\frac{2dzg^{\iota}_{z}}{g_{z}}\bar{G}_{3,z}({e}_{\iota}),

(6.45)

which, together with
\Xi^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})}_{\alpha,z}(0)=0
, shows the bounds in (
5.40
) for
x=0
, as well as (
5.41
). Using the same reasoning we obtain

\displaystyle\Xi^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}_{\alpha,z}({e}_{1})\leq

\displaystyle\frac{g_{z}^{\iota}}{g_{z}}\Big(2\mathscr{B}_{3,\underline{1}}({e}_{1},0)+\sum_{v}\mathscr{T}_{\underline{1},1,1}({e}_{1},v,0)\Big),

(6.46)

which, together with
\Xi^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}_{\alpha,z}({e}_{1})\geq 0
, implies (
5.42
).

To prove (
5.40
) for
x={e}_{1}
, we now show that
\Xi^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})}_{\alpha,z}({e}_{1})<\Xi^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}_{\alpha,z}({e}_{1})
. We do this by arguing that each LA contributing to
\Xi^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})}_{\alpha,z}({e}_{1})
can be decomposed into a unique contribution to
\Xi^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}_{\alpha,z}({e}_{1})
. For any LA that contributes to
\Xi^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})}_{\alpha,z}({e}_{1})
, choose one of the connections between
0
and
{e}_{1}
to be the backbone, and allocate the rest of the LA to be sausages of the walk in any unique way you want (as long as it is unique, the map will yield an injection). As there is a double connection in the original LA, the first and last sausages in the created sausage-walks intersect, and thus provide a contribution to
\Xi^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}_{\alpha,z}({e}_{1})
.
The bounds (
5.45
)-(
5.46
) are obtained in a similar way.

In
\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},1}_{\alpha,{\scriptscriptstyle I},z}({e}_{1}+{e}_{\iota})
and
\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})},1}_{\alpha,{\scriptscriptstyle I},z}({e}_{1}+{e}_{\iota})
, the origin and
{e}_{1}+{e}_{\iota}
are connected using two disjoint paths, each consisting of two bonds.
Thus,

\displaystyle 0\leq\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},1}_{\alpha,{\scriptscriptstyle I},z}({e}_{1}+{e}_{\iota})\leq

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}^{a}\mathscr{B}_{2,\underline{2}}({e}_{1}+{e}_{\iota},0),

(6.47)

\displaystyle 0\leq\Psi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})},1}_{\alpha,{\scriptscriptstyle I},z}({e}_{1}+{e}_{\iota})\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{w_{1}}\mathscr{T}_{0,0,\underline{2}}(w_{1},{e}_{1}+{e}_{\iota},0).

(6.48)

Combining these two bounds yields (
5.43
) and (
5.44
).
∎

7
Proof of main results: Critical exponents
\nu,\gamma
and
\eta

In this section, we prove our main results. We start by noting that Theorem
1.1
immediately follows from the fact that
f_{2}(z)\leq\gamma_{2}

uniformly
in
z<z_{c}
. This implies Theorem
1.1
. Theorem
1.2
follows from the numerical estimates and the facts that
f_{i}(z)\gamma_{i}
uniformly in
z<z_{c}
for
i=1,2
. We prove Corollary
1.3
in Section
7.1
, Theorem
1.4
in Section
7.2
, and Theorem
1.5
in Section
7.3
.

7.1
Proof of
\gamma=1/2
and
\nu=1/4
in Corollary
1.3

The finiteness of the square diagram follows from the infrared bound in Theorem
1.1
, together with the Fourier inversion theorem, which together imply that

\square(z_{c})=\lim_{z\nearrow z_{c}}\square(z)=\lim_{z\nearrow z_{c}}\bar{G}^{\star 4}_{z}(0)=\lim_{z\nearrow z_{c}}\int_{-[\pi,\pi]^{d}}\hat{\bar{G}}_{z}(k)^{4}\frac{d^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}<\infty.

(7.1)

The finiteness of the integral being valid for
d>8
, under the infrared bound which holds for
d\geq 16
and
d\geq 17
for lattice trees and lattice animals, respectively, by Theorem
1.1
. The fact that
\gamma=1/2
follows directly from the finiteness of the square diagram, as proved in
[
4
,
43
,
22
]
.

In the following, we will show that
\nu=1/4
. The critical exponent
\nu
governs the explosion for
z\nearrow z_{c}
of
\xi_{2}(z)
defined in (
1.8
).
For this we rely on notation and techniques used in the accompanying paper
[
14
]
. Indeed, we write, using (
2.12
),

\displaystyle\hat{G}_{z}(k)=\frac{\hat{\Phi}_{z}(k)}{1-\hat{F}_{z}(k)},

(7.2)

with

\displaystyle\hat{\Phi}_{z}(k):=

\displaystyle 1+\hat{\Xi}_{z}(k)-\mu_{z}({\vec{1}}+\vec{\hat{\Psi}}_{z}(k))^{T}\left[{\bf\hat{D}}(k)+\mu_{z}{\bf J}+\hat{\bf\Pi}(k)\right]^{-1}\vec{\hat{\Xi}}_{z}(k),

(7.3)

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{z}(k):=

\displaystyle\mu_{z}({\vec{1}}+\vec{\hat{\Psi}}_{z}(k))^{T}\left[{\bf\hat{D}}(k)+\mu_{z}{\bf J}+\hat{\bf\Pi}(k)\right]^{-1}{\vec{1}}.

(7.4)

By the completion of the bootstrap argument, we know that all the above NoBLE coefficients are well defined for all
z\leq z_{c}
.

In
[
14
, Section 3.3.1]
, we define the continuous Laplace operator
\bigtriangleup
: For a differentiable function
g
and
s\in\{1,2,\dots,d\}
, let
\partial_{s}g(k)=\frac{\partial}{\partial k_{s}}g(k)
and
\bigtriangleup g(k)=\sum_{s=1}^{d}\partial_{s}^{2}g(k)
.
For the Laplace operator
\bigtriangleup
, we note that

\displaystyle\sum_{x}\sum_{A\ni 0,x}|x|^{2}z^{|A|}=-\bigtriangleup\hat{G}_{z}(k)\big|_{k=0},

(7.5)

and compute, now restricting to
z<z_{c}
,

\displaystyle\bigtriangleup\hat{G}_{z}(k)\big|_{k=0}=

\displaystyle\frac{\bigtriangleup\hat{\Phi}_{z}(k)|_{k=0}}{1-\hat{F}_{z}(0)}+\frac{\hat{\Phi}_{z}(0)\bigtriangleup\hat{F}_{z}(k)|_{k=0}}{(1-\hat{F}_{z}(0))^{2}}

(7.6)

\displaystyle+2\frac{\sum_{s}(\partial_{s}\hat{\Phi}_{z}(k)\big|_{k=0})^{2}}{(1-\hat{F}_{z}(0))^{3}}+2\frac{\sum_{s}\partial_{s}\hat{\Phi}_{z}(k)\big|_{k=0}\partial_{s}\hat{F}_{z}(k)\big|_{k=0}}{(1-\hat{F}_{z}(0))^{2}}.

By the symmetry inherited from the symmetry of lattice trees and lattice animals, we know that all first order derivatives are zero at
k=0
, so that all elements in the second line are zero. Thus, we conclude that

\displaystyle\bigtriangleup\hat{G}_{z}(k)\big|_{k=0}=

\displaystyle\frac{\bigtriangleup\hat{\Phi}_{z}(k)|_{k=0}}{\hat{\Phi}_{z}(0)}\chi(z)+\frac{\bigtriangleup\hat{F}_{z}(k)|_{k=0}}{\hat{\Phi}_{z}(0)}\chi(z)^{2}.

(7.7)

Starting from
\xi_{2}(z)
, defined in (
1.8
), and (
7.5
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\xi_{2}(z)^{2}=

\displaystyle-\frac{\bigtriangleup\hat{F}_{z}(k)|_{k=0}}{\hat{\Phi}_{z}(0)}\chi(z)-\frac{\bigtriangleup\hat{\Phi}_{z}(k)|_{k=0}}{\hat{\Phi}_{z}(0)}

(7.8)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\chi(z)\frac{\sum_{x}|x|^{2}F_{z}(x)}{\hat{\Phi}_{z}(0)}+\frac{\sum_{x}|x|^{2}\Phi_{z}(x)}{\hat{\Phi}_{z}(0)}.

Our numerical estimates imply numerical lower and upper bounds of these factors in dimensions above
16
and
17
for lattice trees and lattice animals, respectively, uniformly in
z<z_{c}
. In
[
14
, Appendix D]
we give a detailed description on how to bound such factors using the bounds on the coefficients summarized in
Section
5.3
. In particular, we obtain that, since
\sum_{x}|x|^{2}F_{z}(x)
and
\sum_{x}|x|^{2}\Phi_{z}(x)
are power-series with radius of convergence
z_{c}
, by Abel’s theorem,

\lim_{z\nearrow z_{c}}\sum_{x}|x|^{2}F_{z}(x)=\sum_{x}|x|^{2}F_{z_{c}}(x),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{z\nearrow z_{c}}\sum_{x}|x|^{2}\Phi_{z}(x)=\sum_{x}|x|^{2}\Phi_{z_{c}}(x),

(7.9)

and these sums are absolutely convergent. We thus conclude that

\displaystyle\lim_{z\nearrow z_{c}}\frac{\xi_{2}(z)^{2}}{\chi(z)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{x}|x|^{2}F_{z_{c}}(x)}{\hat{\Phi}_{z_{c}}(0)}.

(7.10)

The fact that
\nu=1/4
now follows from the fact that
\gamma=1/2
.
∎

7.2
Proof of
\eta=0
in
k
-space in Theorem
1.4

By Abel’s theorem, and the fact that
1-\hat{F}_{z_{c}}(k)>0
for
k\neq 0
(which follows from Theorem
1.1
),

\displaystyle\hat{G}_{z_{c}}(k)=\frac{\hat{\Phi}_{z_{c}}(k)}{1-\hat{F}_{z_{c}}(k)}.

(7.11)

Note that
1-\hat{F}_{z_{c}}(0)=0
(which follows from the finiteness of
\hat{\Phi}_{z_{c}}(0)
and the fact that
\chi(z)
blows up as
z\nearrow z_{c}
). Since
\bigtriangleup\hat{F}_{z}(k)|_{k=0}
is well-defined, we thus immediately conclude that

\displaystyle\hat{G}_{z_{c}}(k)=-\frac{\hat{\Phi}_{z_{c}}(0)}{|k|^{2}\bigtriangleup\hat{F}_{z_{c}}(k)|_{k=0}}(1+o(1)),

(7.12)

so that Theorem
1.4
follows with

\bar{A}(d)=-\frac{\hat{\Phi}_{z_{c}}(0)}{\bigtriangleup\hat{F}_{z_{c}}(k)|_{k=0}}.

(7.13)

∎

7.3
Proof of
\eta=0
in
x
-space in Theorem
1.5

The proof of Theorem
1.5
follows by using the
x
-space asymptotics proved by Takashi Hara in
[
15
]
. See in particular
[
15
, Proposition 1.3]
.
For ease of reference, we also mention some useful bounds that follow from our analysis of LAs in dimension
d=17
. We only give the bounds for LAs, as these bounds also bound the corresponding lace-expansion coefficients for LTs. While we only prove Theorem
1.5
for
d\geq 27
, these bounds suggest that the classical lace expansion converges for
d\geq 17
by our numerical estimates.

Using the bound on
f_{2}(z_{c})
directly, with
\bar{A}_{2}(17)=3.78,
we obtain the rough bounds

\displaystyle\bar{G}_{z_{c}}^{\star 4}(0)-1

\displaystyle\leq 292,\qquad\sup_{x\neq 0}\bar{G}_{z_{c}}^{\star 4}(x)\leq 45.

(7.14)

In the following, we improve these rough bounds using the ideas explained in detail in Section
4.1
.

For this, we explain how to modify the bounds on the lace-expansion coefficients for the classical expansion, by allocating one-point functions efficiently, and repulsiveness of the corresponding diagrams. We emphasize that we apply all these bounds to the coefficients of the classical lace expansion, not to the NoBLE, to make them compatible with the classical lace expansion that
[
15
]
is based upon. Allocating the one-point functions properly, and avoiding overcounting them as described in Section
4.2
, we obtain

\displaystyle\tilde{G}_{z_{c}}^{\star 4}(0)-1

\displaystyle\leq 2.777,\qquad\sup_{x\neq 0}\tilde{G}_{z_{c}}^{\star 4}(x)\leq 0.5924.

(7.15)

Next, we use repulsiveness as defined in Section
4.3
. Recall that repulsiveness implies that all paths of the square that actually connect the five (for open squares) or four (for closed squares) boundary points are mutually bond-disjoint. We can bound the squares arising as bounds on intermediate diagrams in the coefficients of the classical lace expansion by

\displaystyle\sum_{w,u,y}\mathscr{S}_{1,0,0,0}(w,u,y,0)

\displaystyle\leq 0.2247,\qquad\sup_{x\neq 0}\sum_{w,u,y}\mathscr{S}_{1,0,0,0}(w,u,y,x)\leq 0.0798561.

(7.16)

We further obtain bounds on the (open and closed) weighted triangles
\bar{T}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2,0)}=\sup_{y}T^{\scriptscriptstyle(2,0)}(y)
, as required for
[
15
]
, given by

\displaystyle T^{\scriptscriptstyle(2,0)}(0)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x}|x|^{2}G_{z}(x)(\tilde{G}_{z}\star\tilde{G}_{z})(x)\leq 2.09182,

(7.17)

\displaystyle\sup_{y\neq 0}T^{\scriptscriptstyle(2,0)}(y)

\displaystyle=\sup_{y\neq 0}\sum_{x}|x|^{2}G_{z}(x)(\tilde{G}_{z}\star\tilde{G}_{z})(x-y)\leq 0.456256.

In communication with Takashi Hara early 2019, we learned that finiteness of the diagrams in (
7.17
), together with the maximum of the diagrams in (
7.16
) being strictly smaller than
\tfrac{1}{2}
, suffices for the
x
-space bounds in Theorem
1.5
to hold. The reason for the latter is that the base of the geometric convergence of the classical lace-expansion coefficients is at most twice the maximum of the squares in (
7.16
). When this number if strictly smaller than one, the classical lace expansion converges, which is needed to apply
[
15
]
. These bounds also hold in any
d\geq 17
, using a numerical check in dimensions
17-29
, and the monotonicity arguments in Appendix
B
for
d\geq 30
. The fact that we only obtain Theorem
1.5
for
d>27
is due to a technical restriction in the proof in
[
15
]
.

The fact that the constant
A(d)
in Theorem
1.5
equals the one in Theorem
1.4
follows directly from the proof in
[
15
]
.
∎

Appendix A
Lower bound on the initial point

In this section we prove that
z_{I}\geq(2d-1)^{-1}{\mathrm{e}}^{-1}
by proving upper bounds on
g_{z_{I}}
and
g^{\iota}_{z_{I}}
:

Lemma A.1

(Lower bound on
z_{I}
)
.

Let
z_{I}
satisfy

z_{I}g_{z_{I}}^{\iota}=\frac{1}{2d-1}.

(A.1)

Then,
z_{I}\geq(2d-1)^{-1}{\mathrm{e}}^{-1}
.

To prove Lemma
A.1
, we use a similar argument as in
[
18
, Proof of Lemma 3.1]
. Unfortunately, one step in
[
18
, Proof of Lemma 3.1]
is not correct as claimed. We correct the argument by using Lemma
A.2
below. We explain the mistake of
[
18
, Proof of Lemma 3.1]
in the proof of Lemma
A.2
.

The proof of Lemma
A.1
relies on the following lemma, that we prove thereafter, and which is of independent interest:

Lemma A.2

(Upper bound on the number of
n
-bond LAs)
.

The number of
n
-bond LAs that contain the origin is bounded above by

\displaystyle 2d(2d-1)^{n-1}\frac{(n+1)^{n}}{(n+1)!}.

(A.2)

Let us first use Lemma
A.2
to prove Lemma
A.1
:

Proof of Lemma
A.1
.

We use Lemma
A.2
to show that
z_{I}\geq(2d-1)^{-1}{\mathrm{e}}^{-1}
.
For this, we bound
g_{z}
using (
A.2
), as

\displaystyle g_{z}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}z^{n}(2d)(2d-1)^{n-1}\frac{(n+1)^{n}}{(n+1)!}.

(A.3)

We note that

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{n^{n-1}}{n!}{\mathrm{e}}^{-n}=1,

(A.4)

since the summand in (
A.4
) equals the probability that the total progeny of a branching process with Poisson offspring distribution with parameter one equals
n
, and the sum of these probabilities equals 1, since the tree is a.s. finite.
We obtain for
z\leq((2d-1){\mathrm{e}})^{-1}
that

\displaystyle g_{z}\leq

\displaystyle 1+\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}z^{n-1}(2d)(2d-1)^{n-2}\frac{n^{n-1}}{n!}\leq 1+\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{(2d)(2d-1)^{n-2}}{(2d-1)^{n-1}{\mathrm{e}}^{n-1}}\frac{n^{n-1}}{n!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+\frac{2d{\mathrm{e}}}{(2d-1)}\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{n^{n-1}}{n!}{\mathrm{e}}^{-n}=1+\frac{2d{\mathrm{e}}}{(2d-1)}\left(1-\frac{1}{{\mathrm{e}}}\right)={\mathrm{e}}+\frac{{\mathrm{e}}-1}{2d-1}.

(A.5)

Since
g^{\iota}_{z}\leq g_{z}
this is also a bound for
g^{\iota}_{z}
. We can improve this bound on
g^{\iota}_{z}
by using the condition that
{e}_{\iota}
is not part of the tree/animal. A non-trivial LT/LA contains at least one edge connecting the origin to a neighboring point.
Therefore, at least at
1
out of
2d
LTs/LAs counted in
g_{z}
does not contribute to
g^{\iota}_{z}
. We conclude that

\displaystyle g^{\iota}_{z}\leq 1+\frac{2d-1}{2d}(g_{z}-1)=1+\frac{(2d-1)({\mathrm{e}}-1)}{2d}\left(1+\frac{1}{2d-1}\right)={\mathrm{e}},

(A.6)

and thus, for all
z\leq z_{I}
,

\displaystyle(2d-1)g^{\iota}_{z}z

\displaystyle\leq 1,\

\displaystyle(2d-1)\bar{\alpha}_{z}=(2d-1)g_{z}z\leq 1+\frac{1-{\mathrm{e}}^{-1}}{2d-1},

(A.7)

as well as, by the definition of
z_{I}
in (
3.55
),

\displaystyle 1=(2d-1)g^{\iota}_{z_{I}}{z_{I}}\leq(2d-1){\mathrm{e}}z_{I}.

(A.8)

This implies that
z_{I}\geq(2d-1)^{-1}{\mathrm{e}}^{-1}
.
∎

We complete this section by proving Lemma
A.2
:

Proof of Lemma
A.2
.

The proof of
[
18
, Lemma 3.1]
uses a similar bound as stated in (
A.2
) in Lemma
A.2
, but contains an error.
Namely it is used that there are
(n+1)^{n}/(n+1)!
abstract unlabeled rooted trees with
n
edges. However, while Cayley’s theorem states that there are
(n+1)^{n}
labeled rooted trees, the removal of the labels does not create a factor
1/(n+1)!
as stated. Indeed, there are less than
(n+1)!
ways to label an abstract tree, e.g., for a tree with two edges there are only
3
different ways to label the tree as the only difference is the label of the vertex that is part of two edges.

To prove the bound stated in (
A.2
) we first use the techniques of
[
3
, Sections 2 and 5]
to show the lemma only for LTs. Then, we adapt the arguments to LAs.

We begin by defining a non-backtracking branching random walk with Poisson offspring distribution. Abstract trees are the family trees of a critical branching process with Poisson offspring distribution. In more detail, we begin with a single individual having
\xi
offspring, where
\xi
is a Poisson random variable of mean
1
, i.e.,
\prob(\xi=m)=({\mathrm{e}}m!)^{-1}
. Each of the offspring then independently has offspring of its own with the same critical Poisson distribution.
We denote by
|\mathcal{T}|
the number of bonds of the tree
\mathcal{T}
. For an abstract rooted tree
\mathcal{T}
,
with the
i
th individual having
\xi_{i}
offspring, this associates to
\mathcal{T}
the weight

\displaystyle\prob(\mathcal{T})={\mathrm{e}}^{-(|\mathcal{T}|+1)}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{T}}\frac{1}{\xi_{i}!}.

(A.9)

We define an embedding of
\varphi
of
\mathcal{T}
into
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
to be a mapping of the vertices of
\mathcal{T}
into
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, such that the root is mapped to the origin and adjacent vertices in the tree are mapped to nearest-neighbors in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Further, we restrict to embeddings in which the children are not mapped to the location of the grandparents. We define the pair
(\mathcal{T},\varphi)
to be a non-backtracking branching random walk with Poisson offspring distribution (Poisson-NBBRW). Given an abstract tree
\mathcal{T}
with
n
bonds, there are
2d(2d-1)^{n-1}
possible embeddings
\varphi
of
\mathcal{T}
. The Poisson-NBBRW measure is given by

\displaystyle\prob(\mathcal{T},\varphi)=\frac{1}{2d(2d-1)^{|\mathcal{T}|-1}}\prob(\mathcal{T})=\frac{1}{2d(2d-1)^{|\mathcal{T}|-1}}{\mathrm{e}}^{-(|\mathcal{T}|+1)}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{T}}\frac{1}{\xi_{i}!}.

(A.10)

for all
\mathcal{T}
and
\varphi
. We define
\Ibold^{\scriptscriptstyle(t)}(\mathcal{T},\varphi)
to be the indicator that the embedding
\varphi
of
\mathcal{T}
is a LT, i.e.,
\varphi\colon\mathcal{T}\mapsto\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is injective. For a LT
T
, we write
T=\varphi(\mathcal{T})
if the embedding
\varphi
of
\mathcal{T}
equals
T
.
For abstract trees with
n
bonds, we define the measure

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Q}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(t)}_{n}(\mathcal{T},\varphi)=\frac{1}{Z^{\scriptscriptstyle(t)}_{n}}\prob(\mathcal{T},\varphi)\Ibold^{\scriptscriptstyle(t)}(\mathcal{T},\varphi),

(A.11)

with normalization

\displaystyle Z^{\scriptscriptstyle(t)}_{n}=\sum_{(\mathcal{T},\varphi)\colon|\mathcal{T}|=n}\prob(\mathcal{T},\varphi)\Ibold^{\scriptscriptstyle(t)}(\mathcal{T},\varphi).

(A.12)

We prove that the number of
n
-bond LTs that contain the origin is given by

\displaystyle t^{\scriptscriptstyle(t)}_{n}(0)=Z^{\scriptscriptstyle(t)}_{n}{\mathrm{e}}^{n+1}2d(2d-1)^{n-1},

(A.13)

which is equivalent to

Z^{\scriptscriptstyle(t)}_{n}=t^{\scriptscriptstyle(t)}_{n}(0){\mathrm{e}}^{-(n+1)}(2d)^{-1}(2d-1)^{-(n-1)}.

(A.14)

To do that, we show that, for each
n
-bond LT
T
,

\displaystyle\sum_{(\mathcal{T},\varphi)\colon\varphi(\mathcal{T})=T}{\mathbb{Q}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(t)}_{n}(\mathcal{T},\varphi)=\frac{{\mathrm{e}}^{-(n+1)}}{2d(2d-1)^{n-1}},

(A.15)

which implies that the measure
{\mathbb{Q}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(t)}_{n}
corresponds to the uniform measure on all
n
-bond LTs and thus implies (
A.14
), and thus (
A.13
).

From (
A.10
) we conclude that, for all
T
with
|T|=n
,

\displaystyle\sum_{(\mathcal{T},\varphi)\colon\varphi(\mathcal{T})=T}\prob(\mathcal{T},\varphi)=\frac{{\mathrm{e}}^{-(n+1)}}{2d(2d-1)^{n-1}}\sum_{(\mathcal{T},\varphi)\colon\varphi(\mathcal{T})=T}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{T}}\frac{1}{\xi_{i}!}.

(A.16)

We continue by proving that, for any LT
T
,

\displaystyle\sum_{(\mathcal{T},\varphi)\colon\varphi(\mathcal{T})=T}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{T}}\frac{1}{\xi_{i}!}=1.

(A.17)

Let
d_{0}
be the degree of
0
in
T
, and given non-zero
x\in T
, let
d_{x}
be the degree of
x
in
T

minus
1
. The set
\{d_{x}\colon x\in T\}
must be equal to the set of
\xi_{i}
for any
\mathcal{T}
that can be mapped to
T
.
Defining
\nu(T)=\#\{(\mathcal{T},\varphi)\colon\varphi(\mathcal{T})=T\}
, (
A.17
) is therefore equivalent to

\displaystyle\nu(T)=\prod_{x\in T}d_{x}!.

(A.18)

We prove (
A.18
) by induction on the number
N
of generations of
T
. By this, we mean the length of the longest self-avoiding path in
T
, starting from the origin. The identity (
A.18
) clearly holds for
N=0
. Our induction hypothesis is that (
A.18
) holds if there are
N-1
or fewer generations. Suppose
T
has
N
generations, let
T_{1},\dots,T_{d_{0}}
denote the LTs resulting from deleting from
T
all bonds incident on the origin. We regard each
T_{a}
as rooted at the neighbor of the origin in the corresponding deleted bond. It suffices to show that
\nu(T)=b_{0}!\prod_{a=1}^{d_{0}}\nu(T_{a})
, since each
T_{a}
has fewer than
N
generations. To prove this, we note that each
(\mathcal{T},\varphi)
with
\varphi(\mathcal{T})=T
induces a Poisson-NBBRW
(\mathcal{T}_{a},\varphi_{a})
such that
\varphi_{a}(\mathcal{T}_{a})=T_{a}
. This correspondence is
d_{0}!
to
1
, since
(\mathcal{T},\varphi)
is determined by the set of
(\mathcal{T}_{a},\varphi_{a})
, up to permutations of the branches of
\mathcal{T}
at its root. This proves
\nu(T)=d_{0}!\prod_{a=1}^{d_{0}}\nu(T_{a})
.

We finally complete the proof of the lemma for LTs. We rearrange (
A.13
) and use (
A.10
) to obtain

\displaystyle t^{\scriptscriptstyle(t)}_{n}(0)=

\displaystyle 2d(2d-1)^{n-1}{\mathrm{e}}^{n+1}\sum_{T\colon|T|=n}\sum_{(\mathcal{T},\varphi)\colon\varphi(\mathcal{T})=T}\prob(\mathcal{T},\varphi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathrm{e}}^{n+1}\sum_{T\colon|T|=n}\sum_{(\mathcal{T},\varphi)\colon\varphi(\mathcal{T})=T}\prob(\mathcal{T})\leq{\mathrm{e}}^{n+1}\sum_{(\mathcal{T},\varphi)\colon|\mathcal{T}|=n}\prob(\mathcal{T}).

(A.19)

Since there are
2d(2d-1)^{n-1}
embeddings
\varphi
, we then know that

\displaystyle t^{\scriptscriptstyle(t)}_{n}(0)\leq

\displaystyle 2d(2d-1)^{n-1}{\mathrm{e}}^{n+1}\sum_{\mathcal{T}\colon|\mathcal{T}|=n}\prob(\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2d(2d-1)^{n-1}{\mathrm{e}}^{n+1}\prob(|\mathcal{T}|=n).

(A.20)

The probability distribution of the total number of bonds of a Poisson branching process is given by
\prob(|\mathcal{T}|=n)={\mathrm{e}}^{-(n+1)}(n+1)^{n}/(n+1)!

(as this is the number of vertices, also called the total progeny, minus 1). We insert this into (
A.20
) and obtain the claimed bound for LTs.

The claim for LAs is obtained using similar ideas, but requires some adaptations as LAs can contain loops. The main difficulty is to obtain a relation similar to (
A.18
).

We define
\Ibold^{\scriptscriptstyle(a)}(\mathcal{T},\varphi)
to be the indicator for the event that

(1)

no two bonds of the abstract tree
\mathcal{T}
are mapped to the same bond in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
by
\varphi
,

(2)

for all
i\in\mathcal{T}
either
\xi_{i}=0
or the following two conditions hold:

(a)

there exist no
j\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\{i\}
, such that
\varphi(i)=\varphi(j),\xi_{j}>0
and
{\rm height}(j)={\rm height}(i)
,

(b)

there exist no
j\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\{i\}
, such that
\varphi(i)=\varphi(j)
and
{\rm height}(j)<{\rm height}(i)
,

where the height of a individual point
a\in\mathcal{T}
is intrinsic distance of
a
to the root in
\mathcal{T}
.

For a Poisson-NBBRW
(\mathcal{T}_{a},\varphi_{a})
(1) guarantees that each bond is only used once by the process and (2) is the condition that whenever a point is visited by multiple individuals then only the first of them can have offspring. Thus, (1) and (2) together imply that the image
\varphi(\mathcal{T})
is a LA. Here, for a LA
A
, we write
A=\varphi(\mathcal{T})
if the embedding
\varphi
of
\mathcal{T}
equals
A
.

Now we proceed as for the LT by defining

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Q}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(a)}_{n}(\mathcal{T},\varphi)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z^{\scriptscriptstyle(a)}_{n}}\prob(\mathcal{T},\varphi)\Ibold^{\scriptscriptstyle(a)}(\mathcal{T},\varphi),

(A.21)

where

Z^{\scriptscriptstyle(a)}_{n}=\sum_{(\mathcal{T},\varphi)\colon|\mathcal{T}|=n}\prob(\mathcal{T},\varphi)\Ibold^{\scriptscriptstyle(a)}(\mathcal{T},\varphi).

(A.22)

We next prove that the number of
n
-bond LAs containing the origin is given by

\displaystyle t^{\scriptscriptstyle(a)}_{n}(0)=Z^{\scriptscriptstyle(a)}_{n}{\mathrm{e}}^{n+1}2d(2d-1)^{n-1}.

(A.23)

This is analogous to the proof for LT in (
A.13
), which the exception of the proof that

\displaystyle\sum_{(\mathcal{T},\varphi)\colon\varphi(\mathcal{T})=A}\Ibold^{\scriptscriptstyle(a)}(\mathcal{T},\varphi)\prod_{i\in\mathcal{T}}\frac{1}{\xi_{i}!}=1.

(A.24)

We now explain how to adapt the argument so as to obtain (
A.24
). To prove this we define
d_{x}
such that it corresponds to the number of offspring of the Poisson branching process. In contrast to the LT case, for LAs it can happen that
\varphi
maps multiple individuals of
\mathcal{T}
to
x
. Therefore, it is not obvious how to choose
d_{x}
such that
(\xi_{i})_{i}
and
(d_{x})_{x}
are equivalent.

For a LA
A
, and two vertices
x,y\in A
, we define
d_{A}(x,y)
to be the intrinsic distance between
x
and
y
, and call the distance
d_{A}(0,x)
the
age
of
x
. For
N\in\Nbold
, we call the set of all vertices with age
N
the
generation

N
. We define an exploration process, that we use to define
(d_{x})_{x}
, as follows:

1)

We define
d_{0}
to be the degree of
0
in
A
;

2)

We define
S
to be the set of all vertices directly connected to the origin in
A
;

3)

We define
B\subset A
to be the subset of all bonds in
A
that do not contain the origin;

4)

Let
N
be the age of the youngest vertex in
S
;

5)

For the unique
x
in the
N
th generation with the lowest lexicographic order:

i)

we define
d_{x}
to be the degree of
x
in
B
;

ii)

we update
S
by adding the endpoints of the bonds in
B
adjacent to
x
, and give these endpoints an age that is equal to the age of
x
plus one;

iii)

we remove
x
from
S
and the bonds that contain
x
from
B
.

6)

If
S
is non-empty, then we repeat the procedure starting in 4) with the updated sets
S
and
B
.

This procedure terminates when
d_{x}
has been defined for all vertices of
A
.

Similarly to the LT case, we define
\nu(A)=\#\{(\mathcal{T},\varphi):\varphi(\mathcal{T})=A,\Ibold^{\scriptscriptstyle(a)}(\mathcal{T},\varphi)=1\}
and now show that

\displaystyle\nu(A)=\prod_{x\in A}d_{x}!,

(A.25)

which implies (
A.24
).

We again prove (
A.25
) by induction on the number
N
of generations of
A
.
The number of generations corresponds to the age of the oldest particle in
A
. The identity (
A.18
)
clearly holds for
N=0
. Our induction hypothesis is that (
A.18
) holds if there are
N-1
or fewer generations.

For a LT, the removal of the
d_{0}
bonds adjacent to the origin splits the tree into
d_{0}
unique, non-intersecting subtrees
T_{1},\dots,T_{d_{0}}
.
Due to possible double connections in a LA, the removal of the bonds adjacent to the origin does not automatically split the animal into
d_{0}
uniquely defined sub-animals
A_{a}
. We will now create a unique split of the animal
A
, that is consistent with the exploration process that we have used to define
(d_{x})_{x}
.

For this, we first remove a certain set of bonds to create a LT
T\subseteq A
. Then we split the
T
into
d_{0}
subtrees as done above. In the last step we add each of the removed bonds to the subtrees to create a unique decomposition
A_{1},\dots,A_{d_{0}}
. This procedure is visualized in Figure
19
. In what follows, we explain how the set of bonds is being chosen. We fix a LA
A
.

0
\vec{A}
(0,1)
(0,0)
(1,-1)
(1,0)
(1,1)
(2,0)
(1,2)
(2,1)
\vec{T}
\vec{A}_{1}
\vec{A}_{3}
\vec{A}_{4}
\vec{A}_{2}

Figure 19
:
The first picture shows how we orient the bonds of
A
to create
\vec{A}
.
In the second picture we see the LT that is created by the removal of the bonds. The coordinates of the vertices that we compare in step (2) of the algorithm below are indicated. The last picture shows the result of the partition of
\vec{A}\setminus\{0\}
, which is the oriented graph obtained by removing the vertex 0 and all edges incident to it from
\vec{A}
.

(1)

We orient all bonds of
A
away from the origin in the intrinsic distance, see Figure
19
, and denote by
\vec{A}
the created LA of oriented bonds. Thus, the oriented bonds
b=(x,y)
are such that the age of
y
equals that of
x
plus one (equal ages cannot happen due to the bipartite structure of the hypercubic lattice
\Zbold^{d}
);

(2)

For all
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
which have multiple ingoing bonds we remove all ingoing bonds from
\vec{A}
with the exception of the ingoing bond
(b_{i}=(\underline{b}_{i},\overline{b}_{i}=x))_{i}
that contains the starting point
\underline{b}_{i}
with the lowest lexicographic order;

(3)

We denote the resulting LA by
\vec{T}
and the set of all removed oriented bonds by
\vec{S}
;

(4)

We know that
\vec{A}=\vec{T}\cup\vec{S}
and that in fact
\vec{T}
is a LT since step (3) leaves at most one ingoing bond for every
x
(and precisely one for all vertices except for 0). Further, we see that
\vec{T}
contains the same set of vertices as
A
, so that
\vec{T}
can also be considered a spanning tree of the graph
A
;

(5)

We delete from
\vec{T}
all bonds incident to the origin and denote the created LTs by
\vec{T}_{1},\dots,\vec{T}_{d_{0}}
;

(6)

For
i=1,\dots,d_{0}
, we define

\displaystyle\vec{A}_{i}=\vec{T}_{i}\cup\{b\in\vec{S}\mid\underline{b}\in\vec{T}_{i}\},

(A.26)

and
A_{i}
to be the LA obtained by removing the orientation of the bonds of
\vec{A}_{i}
.

We regard
A_{i}
as rooted at the unique vertex in it that was directly connected to the origin in
A
.
We see that for all
i=1,\dots,d_{0}
and
x\in A_{i}
, the number
d_{x}
corresponds to the number of outgoing edges of
x
in
A_{i}
.
We note that for all bonds
b\in\vec{S}\cup\vec{A}_{i}
, the end-vertex
\overline{b}
has no outgoing bonds. This is related to the condition
(2)
of the definition of
\Ibold^{\scriptscriptstyle(a)}(\mathcal{T},\varphi)
.

It suffices to show that
\nu(A)=d_{0}!\prod_{a=1}^{d_{0}}\nu(A_{a})
, since each
A_{i}
has at most
N-1
generations. We note that each
(\mathcal{T},\varphi)
with
\varphi(\mathcal{T})=A
induces a Poisson-NBBRW
(\mathcal{T}_{a},\varphi_{a})
such that
\varphi_{a}(\mathcal{T}_{a})=A_{a}
. This correspondence is
d_{0}!
to
1
, since
(\mathcal{T},\varphi)
is determined by the set of
(\mathcal{T}_{a},\varphi_{a})
, up to permutations of the branches of
\mathcal{T}
at its root. This proves
\nu(A)=d_{0}!\prod_{a=1}^{d_{0}}\nu(A_{a})
. This completes the proof of (
A.23
).

Knowing that (
A.23
) holds, we repeat the steps between (
A.20
) and (
A.19
) for LAs, and obtain the claimed bound on
t^{\scriptscriptstyle(a)}_{n}(0)
, which completes the proof of Lemma
A.2
.
∎

Appendix B
Extension to all dimensions
d\geq 30

In this section we prove that when a modified version of our analysis is applied successfully in some dimension
d^{\prime}
, which we choose to be
d^{\prime}=30
, then this analysis will also succeed in
any
dimension
d\geq d^{\prime}
. For this, we need to modify the analysis somewhat, so that the bounds that apply in dimension
d^{\prime}
will be guaranteed to apply to any dimension
d\geq d^{\prime}
. The basic ideas, that also guide the structure of this appendix, are the following:

(1)

In Appendix
B.1
we prove that all SRW integrals that are used in our bounds for
d\geq d^{\prime}
are monotone decreasing in
d
.

(2)

In Appendix
B.2
we prove that the contributions of short explicit paths within our bounds are all monotone decreasing in
d
.

(3)

In Appendix
B.3
we argue that the first two observations imply that the bounds on all simple diagrams are monotone decreasing in
d
.
This will imply that all bounds on the coefficients
\beta_{\bullet}
, as summarized in Section
5.3
, are monotone decreasing in
d
.

(4)

In Appendix
B.4
we show that, since our bounds on the coefficients are all monotone in the dimension
d
, all bounds on the coefficients arising in the rewrite used in the general analysis
[
12
]
, are also monotone.

(5)

In Appendix
B.5
we describe how the above steps imply that whenever the bootstrap succeeds with a given set of parameters
(\gamma_{i},\Gamma_{i})_{i=1}^{3}

in
d=d^{\prime}
, then it also succeeds in
all
dimension
d\geq d^{\prime}
. This in turn implies our results in all
d\geq d^{\prime}
.

We emphasize that the bounds derived here are slightly worse than the ones used for a specific dimension. This is due to the fact that we do not know that
all
SRW integrals that we rely upon in the bounds for explicit dimensions are monotone decreasing. Thus, we need to rely on the subset of SRW integrals that we can prove to be monotone decreasing.

We implement these bounds, which are provably monotone in
d
, using the notebook
LAmonotone
.
Before starting this, let us clarify the notion of monotonicity in
d
:

Definition B.1

(Monotonicity in dimension)
.

Let
g^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}\colon\Zbold^{d}\rightarrow\Rbold
be a family of functions. Recall that we say that
g^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}(x^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)})
is monotone in
d
when
g^{\scriptscriptstyle(d+1)}((x^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)},0))\leq g^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}(x^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)})
, where
(x^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)},0)=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d},0)
is
x^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}
appended with an extra coordinate that takes the value zero.

Prominent examples of such families are the critical SRW Green’s function in
d
dimensions
C_{1/(2d)}^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}
, for which we investigate its monotonicity for
x^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}
equal to the origin, as well as some points close to the origin.

B.1
SRW Integrals

We start by showing that all the bounds on the SRW integrals
(I_{n,m},K_{n,m},T_{n,m},U_{n})
and
\mathcal{J}_{n,l}(x)
that we use in our analysis can be uniformly bounded in
d
, using bounds that are monotone decreasing in
d
. We emphasize that we do not claim that all integrals, such as
U_{n}
, are monotone decreasing in
d
, only that the bounds that we rely upon are.
The reason for this distinction is that the only monotonicity that we can actually prove is given by the two lemmas below:

Lemma B.2

(Monotonicity of SRW Green’s function in
d
)
.

Let
|x|_{\scriptscriptstyle\infty}
be the supremum norm of
x\in\Zbold^{d}
. Then, for every
n\geq 1
and
x^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}\in\Zbold^{d}
with
|x^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}|_{\scriptscriptstyle\infty}\leq 2
,

I_{n,0}^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}(x^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)})\geq I_{n,0}^{\scriptscriptstyle(d+1)}(x^{\scriptscriptstyle(d+1)}),

(B.1)

where
x^{\scriptscriptstyle(d+1)}_{i}=x^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}_{i}
for
i\in\{1,\dots,d\}
, while
x^{\scriptscriptstyle(d+1)}_{d+1}=0
.

Lemma B.3

(Monotonicity of SRW Green’s function in
x
)
.

For any positive integer
n\geq 1
, the function
x\mapsto I_{n,0}(x)
is monotone decreasing in each
|x|_{\mu}
with
\mu=1,2,\dots,d
.

Lemma
B.3
is
[
19
, Lemma B.3]
and follows from the monotonicity of Bessel functions,
see (
B.3
). Lemma
B.2
is already proven for all
x
with
|x^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}|_{\scriptscriptstyle\infty}\leq 1
, in the proof of
[
19
, Lemma C.1]
. For this reason we copy parts of the proof almost verbatim from
[
19
, Lemma C.1]
. For
|x^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}|_{\scriptscriptstyle\infty}=2
, we only need to add some additional ideas.

Proof.

Equation
[
19
, (B.2)]
, states, after a rescaling of the
t
variable, that

I_{n,0}^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}(x^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)})=\frac{1}{(n-1)!}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\ t^{n-1}\prod_{\mu=1}^{d}f_{|x^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}_{i}|}(t/d)dt,

(B.2)

where

f_{N}(z)=\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}{\mathrm{e}}^{-z[1-\cos(\theta)]}\cos(N\theta).

(B.3)

Let
\ell_{1}
be the number of coordinates equal to
\pm 1
in
x^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}
, and
\ell_{2}
the number of coordinates equal to
\pm 2
in
x^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}
. Then,

I_{n,0}^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}(x^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)})=\frac{1}{(n-1)!}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{n-1}f_{0}(t/d)^{d}\Big(\frac{f_{1}(t/d)}{f_{0}(t/d)}\Big)^{\ell_{1}}\Big(\frac{f_{2}(t/d)}{f_{0}(t/d)}\Big)^{\ell_{2}}dt.

(B.4)

Denoting the
L_{p}(-\pi,\pi)
norm of
g_{s}(\theta):=\exp(-s(1-\cos\theta))
by
\|g_{s}\|_{p}
, we obtain by (
B.3
) that

f_{0}(s/d)^{d}=\|g_{s}\|_{1/d}
, which is monotone decreasing in
d
for fixed
s
. This already yields the statement for
x^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}=0
.

As shown by Hara and Slade
[
19
, Proof of Lemma C.1]
, the factor
f_{1}(s/d)/f_{0}(s/d)
is monotone decreasing in
d
, for fixed
s
, because
z\mapsto f_{1}(z)/f_{0}(z)
is monotone increasing in
z>0
.
This can be seen from a direct calculation of its derivative, which gives

\frac{d}{dz}\Big(\frac{f_{1}(z)}{f_{0}(z)}\Big)=\langle\cos^{2}(\theta)\rangle-\langle\cos(\theta)\rangle^{2}\geq 0,

(B.5)

where

\langle f(\theta)\rangle=\frac{\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\theta\ f(\theta){\mathrm{e}}^{z\cos\theta}}{\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\theta{\mathrm{e}}^{z\cos\theta}}.

(B.6)

This proves the statement for all
x^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}
with
|x^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}|_{\scriptscriptstyle\infty}=1
, and starts the point where we extend
[
19
, Lemma C.1]
.

For even
N
, we use the periodicity of
\cos(\theta)
and
\cos(N\theta)
, as well as
\cos(N\theta)=\cos(N(\theta+\pi))
and (
B.3
), to see that

\displaystyle f_{2}(z)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{e}}^{-z}\int_{-\pi/2}^{3\pi/2}\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}{\mathrm{e}}^{z\cos(\theta)}\cos(N\theta)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{e}}^{-z}\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}\big[\cos(N\theta){\mathrm{e}}^{z\cos(\theta)}+\cos(N(\theta+\pi)){\mathrm{e}}^{z\cos(\theta+\pi)})\big]

\displaystyle={\mathrm{e}}^{-z}\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}\cos(N\theta)({\mathrm{e}}^{z\cos(\theta)}+{\mathrm{e}}^{-z\cos(\theta)})={\mathrm{e}}^{-z}\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\frac{d\theta}{\pi}\cosh(z\cos(\theta))\cos(Nz).

For odd
N
, the same can be derived where
\cosh
is replaced by
\sinh
, as
\cos(N\theta)=-\cos(N(\theta+\pi))
for odd
N
, but we will not rely on this fact.

Using a uniform random variable
U
on
[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
, we can rewrite the above as

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dz}\frac{f_{2}(z)}{f_{0}(z)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\expec[\sinh(z\cos(U))\cos(2U)\cos(U)]}{\expec[\cosh(z\cos(U))]}

(B.7)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{\expec[\cosh(z\cos(U))\cos(2U)]\expec[\sinh(z\cos(U))\cos(U)]}{\expec[\cosh(z\cos(U))]^{2}}.

Using the random variable
X
with density
f_{\scriptscriptstyle X}(z)=\cosh(zx)/\int_{0}^{\pi}\cosh(zy)dy
, we can simplify

\frac{d}{dz}\frac{f_{2}(z)}{f_{0}(z)}=\mathrm{Cov}(\tanh(z\cos(X))\cos(X),\cos(2X)).

(B.8)

Then, we note that
\cos(2x)=2\cos(x)^{2}-1
, so

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dz}\frac{f_{2}(z)}{f_{0}(z)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Cov}(\tanh(z\cos(X))\cos(X),2\cos(X)^{2}-1)

\displaystyle=2\mathrm{Cov}(\tanh(z\cos(X))\cos(X),\cos(X)^{2}).

(B.9)

Finally, let
Y=\cos(X)
, which is non-negative as
X\in[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
, to conclude

\frac{d}{dz}\frac{f_{2}(z)}{f_{0}(z)}=2\mathrm{Cov}(\tanh(zY)Y,Y^{2}).

(B.10)

Since both
y\mapsto\tanh(zy)y
as well as
y\mapsto y^{2}
are increasing on
[0,1]
, the above covariance is non-negative. Here we use that
\mathrm{Cov}(f(Y),g(Y))\geq 0
when both
f
and
g
are increasing on the support of
Y
, with strict inequality when both are not constant.
∎

In what follows,
every
bound on SRW-integrals that we use is concluded from
the monotonicity in
d
of
I_{n,0}(x)
of Lemma
B.2
, as well as the monotonicity in
|x_{\mu}|
of
I_{n,0}(x)
in Lemma
B.3
.
In this way all these bounds will inherit their monotonicity from these
I_{n,0}(x)
.
To compute these other bounds, we use that

I_{n,m}(x)=(D\star I_{n,m-1}(x))=\sum_{\iota}I_{n,m-1}(x+{e}_{\iota}).

(B.11)

and apply this bound iteratively starting from
m=1
until
m=10
to create our bounds.

As we know that
I_{n,m}(x)
is monotone in
d
only for
m=0
and appropriate values of
x
,
it is a priori not clear that these bounds are also monotone decreasing in
d
.
For this reason, let us create a bound that is monotone decreasing by construction.
For
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, let

\displaystyle\underline{x}:=(\underline{x}_{i})_{i=1}^{d},\qquad\text{where}\qquad\begin{cases}\underline{x}_{i}=x_{i}&\text{ when }|x_{i}|\leq 2,\\
\underline{x}_{i}=2&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Thus,
\underline{x}
is the point that is closest to
x
and is still covered by Lemma
B.2
.
By Lemma
B.3
. we know that
I_{n,0}(x)\leq I_{n,0}(\underline{x})
, which allows us to use monotone bounds.

Let us now explain how we use the above ideas to derive monotone bounds on
I_{n,m}(x)
for every
n,m,x
. We define
\bar{I}_{n,0}(x):=I_{n,0}(\underline{x})
and, for
m\geq 1
, define
\bar{I}_{n,m}(x)
recursively by

\displaystyle\bar{I}_{n,m}(x):=\sum_{\iota}\bar{I}_{n,m-1}(x+{e}_{\iota}).

To finalize the bounds we need to show that

\displaystyle I_{n,m}(x)\leq\bar{I}_{n,m}(x),

(B.12)

which we will do by induction on
m
.

For
m=0
, the inequality (
B.12
) holds by definition and Lemma
B.3
.
For
m\geq 1
, we note that

\displaystyle I_{n,m}(x)=(D\star I_{n,m-1}(x))=\sum_{\iota}I_{n,m-1}(x+{e}_{\iota})\leq\sum_{\iota}\bar{I}_{n,m-1}(x+{e}_{\iota})=\bar{I}_{n,m}(x),

where the inequality follows from (
B.12
) for
m-1
. This advances the induction, and thus proves (
B.12
). It also concludes our construction of bounds that are monotone in
d
, as
\bar{I}_{n,m}
is monotone in the sense of Definition
B.1
.

Using these monotone bounds on
I_{n,m}(x)
we conclude bounds on
L_{n}(x)
, as given in
[
12
, (5.17)]
, e.g.

\displaystyle L_{n}({e}_{1}+{e}_{2})=

\displaystyle\frac{(d-2)(d-3)}{d(d-1)}I_{n,0}({e}_{1}+{e}_{2}+{e}_{3}+{e}_{4})

\displaystyle+\frac{d-2}{2d(d-1)}\left(I_{n,0}({e}_{1}+{e}_{2})+I_{n,0}(2{e}_{1}+{e}_{2}+{e}_{3})\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4d(d-1)}\left(I_{n,0}(0)+I_{n,0}(2{e}_{1}+2{e}_{2})+2I_{n,0}(2{e}_{1})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\bar{I}_{n,0}({e}_{1}+{e}_{2}+{e}_{3}+{e}_{4})+\frac{1}{2d}\left(\bar{I}_{n,0}({e}_{1}+{e}_{2})+\bar{I}_{n,0}(2{e}_{1}+{e}_{2})\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4d(d-1)}\left(\bar{I}_{n,0}(0)+I_{n,0}(2{e}_{1}+{e}_{2})+2\bar{I}_{n,0}(2{e}_{1})\right),

which again, by the monotonicity in the coordinates of
I_{n,0}(x)
, creates a bound on
L_{n}(x)
that is monotone decreasing in
d
as well.
Using the same idea and the methods in
[
12
, Section 5.2]
, we can construct bounds on
K_{n,m}(x)
,
T_{n,m}(x)
,
V_{n,m}
,
T_{n,m}(x)
,
\mathcal{J}_{n,l}(x)
.
These bounds inherit their monotonicity in
d
from the
\bar{I}_{n,m}(x)
.
Note that we also remove all terms that are subtracted, e.g.
2I_{n,l}(2{e}_{1})
in
[
12
, (5.25)]
, and
use only points
x
covered by Lemma
B.2
, by exploiting the monotonicity of
\bar{I}_{n,m}(x)
in
x
.
All this can be found in the second section of the (very readable)
LAMonotone
Mathematica notebook,
which can be downloaded from
[
9
]
, in its original form, as well as in pdf-format.

B.2
Monotonicity of small-step paths

A corner stone of our numerical bounding procedure is to extract explicit short contributions from our diagrams,
for which we can use the repulsiveness of connections involved. For this step we require that

\tfrac{c_{n}(x)}{(2d-1)^{n}}

is monotone decreasing in
d
, where
c_{n}
is the size of a collection of paths, such as simple random walk, self-avoiding walk, or bond-self-avoiding walk paths, of
n
steps, starting at the origin and ending at
x\in\Zbold^{d}
. Our argument applies to each of these collections.

Lemma B.4

(Monotonicity of small-step paths)
.

Fix
n
and
x
. Let
c_{n}(x)
be the number of
n
-step paths from 0 to
x
of any type and
\mathrm{dim}(x)
be the dimensionality of
x
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathrm{dim}(x)

\displaystyle:=\#\{\text{\rm{non-zero coordinates of $x$}}\}.

Then,
d\mapsto c_{n}(x)/(2d-1)^{n}
is monotonically decreasing in
d
for

2d\geq\max\{2\mathrm{dim}(x)+n-|x|_{1},2n-2\}
.

Proof.

We define the dimension of a path be the maximal dimension of the points it traverses, and let

\displaystyle c_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}(x)

\displaystyle:=\#\{\text{number of paths from $0$ to $x$ which have exactly $d$ dimension}\}.

This
c_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}
has the following properties, which hold for any type of nearest-neighbor paths:

(i)

c_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}(x)\equiv 0
when
n<|x|_{1}
, as there are simply not enough steps
n
to reach
x

(ii)

c_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}(x)\equiv 0
when
d<\mathrm{dim}(x)
, as the point
x
lives in larger dimension than
d
.

(iii)

c_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}(x)\equiv 0
when
n-|x|_{1}
is odd, by parity of the lattice.

(iv)

c_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle(d)}(x)\equiv 0
when
d>\mathrm{dim}(x)+\tfrac{n-|x|_{1}}{2}
, as the path can simply not use all the required dimensions
d
and still reach
x
in
n
steps.

By grouping the paths from
0
to
x
according to the number of dimensions
d^{\prime}
used, regarding the properties above, we obtain

\displaystyle c_{n}(x)=

\displaystyle\sum_{d^{\prime}=\mathrm{dim}(x)}^{\mathrm{dim}(x)+\tfrac{n-|x|_{1}}{2}}{d-\mathrm{dim}(x)\choose d^{\prime}-\mathrm{dim}(x)}c^{\scriptscriptstyle(d^{\prime})}_{n}(x)=\sum_{d^{\prime}=0}^{\tfrac{n-|x|_{1}}{2}}{d-\mathrm{dim}(x)\choose d^{\prime}}c^{\scriptscriptstyle(d^{\prime}+\mathrm{dim}(x))}_{n}(x).

The binomial coefficient represents the free choices for the additional dimensions
d^{\prime}
, not imposed by
x
itself, that the path uses.
We then write

\frac{c_{n}(x)}{(2d-1)^{n}}=\sum_{d^{\prime}=0}^{\tfrac{n-|x|_{1}}{2}}f_{n}(d^{\prime},d,x)c^{\scriptscriptstyle(d^{\prime}+\mathrm{dim}(x))}_{n}(x),

(B.13)

where

f_{n}(d^{\prime},d,x):=\frac{{d-\mathrm{dim}(x)\choose d^{\prime}}}{(2d-1)^{n}}.

(B.14)

Our restriction to
2d\geq 2\mathrm{dim}(x)+n-|x|_{1}
ensures that each summand contributes, which allows us to conclude the
required monotonicity of
d\mapsto c_{n}(x)/(2d-1)^{n}
by showing that
d\mapsto f_{n}(d^{\prime},d+1)
is monotonically decreasing for every
d^{\prime}
fixed.
For this, we need to show that

\frac{f_{n}(d^{\prime},d+1,x)}{f_{n}(d^{\prime},d,x)}=\frac{d+1-\mathrm{dim}(x)}{d+1-\mathrm{dim}(x)-d^{\prime}}\Big(\frac{2d-1}{2d+1}\Big)^{n},

(B.15)

is smaller than one. The first fraction is monotone increasing in
\mathrm{dim}(x)
and
d^{\prime}
.
We use that
d^{\prime}\leq\tfrac{n-|x|_{1}}{2}
,
\mathrm{dim}(x)\leq|x|_{1}
and then
n\geq|x|_{1}\geq 0
to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d+1-\mathrm{dim}(x)}{d+1-\mathrm{dim}(x)-d^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{d+1-|x|_{1}}{d+1-|x|_{1}-\tfrac{n-|x|_{1}}{2}}=1+\frac{n-|x|_{1}}{2d+2-|x|_{1}-n}

(B.16)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\frac{n}{2d+2-n}=\frac{2d+2}{2d+2-n},

(B.17)

where the final inequality follows since
n\leq d+1
.
For the second fraction we note that
(1+y)^{n}\leq 1+yn+y^{2}n(n-1)/2

for all
n\leq y
and
y>-1
, so that

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{2d-1}{2d+1}\Big)^{n}=\Big(1-\frac{2}{2d-1}\Big)^{n}\leq 1-\frac{2n}{2d-1}+\frac{2n(n-1)}{(2d-1)^{2}}=\frac{(2d-1)^{2}-2n(2d-1)+2n(n-1)}{(2d-1)^{2}}.

Combining both bounds, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{f_{n}(d^{\prime},d+1,x)}{f_{n}(d^{\prime},d,x)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(2d-1)^{2}-2n(2d-1)+2n(n-1)}{(2d-1)^{2}}\frac{2d+2}{2d+2-n}

(B.18)

\displaystyle=1+\frac{n(2d-1)^{2}-2n(2d-1)(2d+2)+n(n-1)(2d+2)}{(2d-1)^{2}(2d+2-n)}

\displaystyle=1+n\frac{(2d-1)^{2}-2(2d-1)(2d+2)+(n-1)(2d+2)}{(2d-1)^{2}(2d+2-n)}.

We finally note that, since
n\leq d
,

\displaystyle(2d-1)^{2}-2(2d-1)(2d+2)+(n-1)(2d+2)

(B.19)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq(2d-1)^{2}-2(2d-1)^{2}+(d-1)(2d+2)=2(d^{2}-1)-(2d-1)^{2}<0,

when
d\geq 1
.

∎

B.3
Monotonicity of bounds on the NoBLE coefficients

We bound simple and repulsive diagrams using the bootstrap assumption,
SRW-integrals and by extracting short explicit contributions. This is explained in detail in
[
12
, Section 5.3.1]
.
For example,

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{1,1,0}(x)\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=2}^{M-1}\frac{(i-1)i}{2}p_{i}(x)(zg^{\iota}_{z})^{i}+\frac{(M-2)(M-3)}{2}(2dzg^{\iota}_{z})^{M}(D^{\star M}\star G_{z})(x)

\displaystyle+(M-2)(2dzg^{\iota}_{z})^{M}(D^{\star M}\star G^{\star 2}_{z})(x)+(2dzg^{\iota}_{z})^{M}(D^{\star M}\star G^{\star 3}_{z})(x),

where
p_{n}
is the number of
n
-step bond-self-avoiding paths from
0
to
x
, i.e., paths that do not use the same bond twice. For dimension
d=16,\dots,29
we use
M=10
. For our analysis in
d\geq 30
, we extract only
7
steps, so that
M=8
.

To obtain numerical values for these bounds, we use

\displaystyle zg^{\iota}_{z}\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2d-1}\Gamma_{1}\quad\text{ and }(D^{\star M}\star G^{\star n}_{z})(x)\leq\big(\frac{2d-2}{2d-1}\Gamma_{2}\big)^{n}K_{n,M}(x)\leq\Gamma_{2}^{n}K_{n,M}(x),

and use the following bound that can be numerically computed:

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{1,1,0}(x)\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=2}^{7}\frac{(i-1)i}{2}\frac{p_{i}(x)}{(2d-1)^{i}}\Gamma^{i}_{1}+15\left(\frac{2d}{2d-1}\Gamma_{1}\right)^{8}\Gamma_{2}K_{1,8}(x)

\displaystyle+6\left(\frac{2d}{2d-1}\Gamma_{1}\right)^{8}\Gamma_{2}^{2}K_{2,8}(x)+\left(\frac{2d}{2d-1}\Gamma_{1}\right)^{8}\Gamma_{2}^{3}K_{3,8}(x).

By Lemma
B.4
the first term is monotone decreasing in
d
. Further, we bound
K_{n,8}(x)
using bounds that are monotone in
d
, see
Section
B.1
, which means that our bound on
\mathscr{T}_{1,1,0}(x)
is monotone decreasing in
d
.

We use the same kind of bounds for
all
simple and repulsive diagrams. As we bound all NoBLE coefficients using these diagrams (recall Section
5.2
),
the bounds that we rely upon inherit this monotonicity. Having made this point we should add that in three bounds in Lemma
5.2
we see a factor of
(2d-2)/2d
,
which is not monotone decreasing. We resolve this non-monotonicity issue by simply replacing
(2d-2)/2d
by
1
in our bounds/implementation for
d\geq 30
.

Unfortunately our verification of the technical condition
\Pi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota,\kappa}(x)\leq zg_{z}\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}(x)
is numerical. We have already proved this for all
x\neq{e}_{\iota}+{e}_{\kappa}
. Even though the contribution for
x={e}_{\iota}+{e}_{\kappa}
is quite small (recall (
3.76
)), we did not manage to find a proof that is uniform in the dimension for
x={e}_{\iota}+{e}_{\kappa}
. This causes problems in the application of the general analysis in
[
12
]
. To bypass this, we use a bound that applies to
\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}
and
\Pi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota,\kappa}
at the same time, and replace
\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle\Xi^{\iota}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\beta_{{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta\Xi^{\iota}},0}^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\beta_{{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta\Xi^{\iota}},\iota}^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})}
that are currently used as to bound
\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota}
only, by a bound that applies also to
\Pi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})},\iota,\kappa}
. Numerically, this makes no difference, as our crude numerical bound already applies to both these terms at the same time.

This problem has repercussions in the proof of (
5.25
) and (
5.26
), where this bound was used.
However, the problem does not arise in (
5.24
), since it does not involve
x={e}_{\iota}+{e}_{\kappa}
.
This problem does not arise in (
5.26
) either, since it is zero when
x={e}_{\iota}+{e}_{\kappa}
(recall also (
6.16
)).

In conclusion, all bounds that are used in the general analysis of
[
12
]
, as summarized in Section
5.3
, are monotone decreasing in
d
.

B.4
Monotonicity of bounds on the general analysis coefficients

In this section we discuss the analysis of the generalized NoBLE analysis as explained in
[
12
]
.
Our aim is to show that the monotonicity of the bounds on the coefficients implies that

if
a bootstrap, as described in Proposition
2.3
, is
successful in the given dimension
d^{\prime}=30
,
then
the bootstrap will be successful in every larger dimension
d\geq d^{\prime}
.
For this, we fix the assumed bounds
\Gamma_{i},c_{n,l,S}
and use them uniformly for all
d\geq 30
. We next explain how we can see that the success of
the bootstrap for
d=30
implies that for
d\geq 30
, splitting between the various bootstrap function
f_{i}(z)
for
i=1,2,3
:

Monotonicity of success for the initialization and improvement of
f_{1}
.

We start with the bootstrap function

\displaystyle f_{1}(z)=\max\left\{(2d-1)zg_{z},c_{\mu}(2d-1)zg_{z}^{\iota}\right\}.

In (
A.7
) we have already seen the bound on
f_{1}(z_{I})
, which is clearly monotone decreasing in
d
. This deals with the initialization.

For
z\in(z_{I},z_{c})
, the bounds on
f_{1}(z)
as given in
[
12
, (3.5)]
can be bounded uniformly in
d
as follows. In our argument, we write

\beta_{\bullet}(d)
for the parameter
\beta_{\bullet}
in dimension
d
. Note that the analysis in the previous section implies the monotonicity of
d\mapsto\beta_{\bullet}(d)
for
all

\beta_{\bullet}(d)
discussed in Section
5.3
. This leads to

\displaystyle f_{1}(z)\leq

\displaystyle\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle\mu}(d)\frac{1+\overline{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Pi^{\iota}}(d)}{1-\frac{2d}{2d-1}\underline{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Psi^{\kappa}}(d)}\leq\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle\mu}(30)\frac{1+\overline{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Pi^{\iota}}(30)}{1-\frac{60}{59}\underline{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Psi^{\kappa}}(30)}.

For
\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle\mu}(d)
, which bounds
g_{z}/g_{z}^{\iota}
, we note that

\displaystyle g_{z}-g_{z}^{\iota}=\bar{G}_{z}({e}_{\iota}).

This implies

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\mu}_{z}}{\mu_{z}}=\frac{g_{z}}{g_{z}^{\iota}}=1+\frac{\bar{G}_{z}({e}_{\iota})}{g_{z}^{\iota}}=1+\frac{\bar{\mu}_{z}}{\mu_{z}}G_{z}({e}_{\iota})\Rightarrow\frac{\bar{\mu}_{z}}{\mu_{z}}=\frac{1}{1-G_{z}({e}_{\iota})}.

We bound
G_{z}({e}_{\iota})
in terms of simple diagrams, see Appendix
B.3
. This
creates a bound that is monotone decreasing. Thus, our bound on
\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle\mu}(d)
is decreasing in
d
as well.

Bounds on the rewrite.

We bound
f_{2}
and
f_{3}
using a rewrite as this made the analysis much clearer.
To proceed we next show that all coefficients of the rewrite are monotone decreasing in
d
.
This is done by checking each bound on the rewrite, as given in
[
12
, Appendix D]
, one bound at a time.

For most bounds it is obvious that they are monotone, as they only use the bounds on the coefficients
\beta_{\bullet}
(which we showed are monotone already in the previous section).
For this reason we only comment on three issues, for which it is not obvious that the bounds are monotone decreasing.

Regarding the bounds on
[
12
, (D.4)]
and
[
12
, (D.5)]
, we note that we have
defined
\underline{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Psi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})}=\underline{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle\sum\Pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})}=0
,
so that the last terms in these lines are not a concern regarding monotonicity.

In the bounds on the remainder terms in
[
12
, Steps 2-5 in Appendix D]
, the following terms appear:

\displaystyle\frac{\mu}{1-\mu^{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\bar{\mu}}{1-\mu^{2}}\leq\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle\mu}\frac{\mu}{1-\mu^{2}}.

We next derive bounds on these terms that are uniform in
d
.

The function
\tfrac{x}{1-x^{2}}
is monotone increasing on the interval
[0,1]
, and

\mu\leq\tfrac{\Gamma_{1}}{2d-1}\leq\tfrac{\Gamma_{1}}{59}
for
d\geq 30
, so that

\displaystyle\frac{\mu}{1-\mu^{2}}\leq\frac{\Gamma_{1}}{(2d-1)(1-\frac{\Gamma_{1}^{2}}{(2d-1)^{2}})}\leq\frac{\tfrac{\Gamma_{1}}{59}}{1-\tfrac{\Gamma_{1}^{2}}{59^{2}}}.

The term
\alpha_{{\scriptscriptstyle F},z}\approx 1
, which plays a central role in the NoBLE rewrite and is defined in
[
12
, (4.18)]
, is bounded from above and below.
We require an upper bound that is decreasing and a lower bound that is increasing in
d
.
The upper bound, stated in
[
12
, (D.2)]
,

\displaystyle\alpha_{{\scriptscriptstyle F},z}\leq\frac{2d\mu}{1-\mu^{2}}\left[1+\beta_{{\scriptscriptstyle\sum\Psi^{\iota}_{\alpha},I}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0-1})}+\mu\beta_{{\scriptscriptstyle\sum\Psi^{\iota}_{\alpha},II}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1-0})}-\frac{1}{1-\mu^{2}}\underline{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle\sum\Pi_{\alpha}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})}\right]

is clearly monotone, as we have chosen
\underline{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle\sum\Pi_{\alpha}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})}=0

and bound the other two terms using repulsive diagrams, see (
5.43
), (
5.46
).
As
\alpha_{{\scriptscriptstyle F},z}
arises in the dominator of
\hat{G}_{z}(k)
, see
[
12
, (1.37)]
,
we require a monotone increasing lower bound on
\alpha_{{\scriptscriptstyle F},z}
,
to obtain then a bound on
\hat{G}_{z}(k)
that is monotone decreasing. We use the lower bound in
[
12
, (D.2)]
that reads

\displaystyle\alpha_{{\scriptscriptstyle F},z}\geq\frac{2d\mu}{1-\mu^{2}}

\displaystyle\left[1-\beta_{{\scriptscriptstyle\sum\Psi^{\iota}_{\alpha},I}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1-0})}-\mu\beta_{{\scriptscriptstyle\sum\Psi^{\iota}_{\alpha},II}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0-1})}-\frac{1}{1-\mu^{2}}\bar{\beta}_{\sum\scriptscriptstyle\Pi_{\alpha}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})}\right].

The term in the brackets is bounded by

\displaystyle 1-\beta_{{\scriptscriptstyle\sum\Psi^{\iota}_{\alpha},I}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1-0})}(d)-\frac{\Gamma_{1}}{(2d-1)}\beta_{{\scriptscriptstyle\sum\Psi^{\iota}_{\alpha},II}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0-1})}(d)-\frac{1}{1-\frac{\Gamma_{1}^{2}}{(2d-1)^{2}}}\bar{\beta}_{\sum\scriptscriptstyle\Pi_{\alpha}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 0})}(d):=1-\zeta(d).

This sum of bounds in
\zeta(d)
is clearly decreasing in
d
, so that
1-\zeta(d)\geq 1-\zeta(30)
.
Let us remark that we have implicitly assume that
\zeta(d)<1
, which a relatively weak condition that we have verified numerically.
The initial factor
\tfrac{2d\mu}{1-\mu^{2}}
is monotone increasing in
\mu
and we know that

\displaystyle\mu=zg^{\iota}_{z}\geq z_{I}g_{z_{I}}^{\iota}=\frac{1}{2d-1},

for all
z\geq z_{I}
, by definition of
z_{I}
. This means that

\displaystyle\frac{2d\mu}{1-\mu^{2}}\geq\frac{2d-1}{2d-2}=1+\frac{1}{2d-2}\geq 1,

so that

\displaystyle\alpha_{{\scriptscriptstyle F},z}\geq\frac{2d-1}{2d-2}\left[1-\zeta(30)\right].

Due to the factor
\tfrac{2d-1}{2d-2}
this is decreasing in
d
and not increasing as required for the lower bounds on

\alpha_{{\scriptscriptstyle F},z}
. We can circumvent by simply using a uniform bound
\tfrac{2d-1}{2d-2}\leq\tfrac{59}{58}
, which we use in the implementation.

Monotonicity of success for the initialization and improvement of
f_{2}
.

The bootstrap function
f_{2}
, defined in (
2.16
), bounds
\hat{G}_{z}(k)
. The bounds for the initialization follow the same bounds as for the improvement, so we only discuss the improvement here.

From
[
12
, (3.9)]
and the bounds on
\alpha_{{\scriptscriptstyle F},z}
that we derived above, we obtain

\displaystyle|\hat{G}_{z}(k)|[1-\hat{D}(k)]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\overline{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle c,\Phi}(d)+\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle|\alpha,\Phi|}(d)+\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle|R,\Phi|}(d)}{\underline{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle\alpha,F}(d)-\underline{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta R,F}(d)}\leq\frac{\overline{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle c,\Phi}(30)+\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle|\alpha,\Phi|}(30)+\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle|R,\Phi|}(30)}{\tfrac{2d-1}{2d-2}\left[1-\zeta(30)\right]-\underline{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta R,F}(30)}.

The definition (
2.16
) states

\displaystyle f_{2}(z)=

\displaystyle\frac{2d-1}{2d-2}\sup_{k\in(-\pi,\pi)^{d}}[1-\hat{D}(k)]\ \hat{G}_{z}(k)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\overline{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle c,\Phi}(30)+\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle|\alpha,\Phi|}(30)+\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle|R,\Phi|}(30)}{\tfrac{2d-2}{2d-1}\left(\tfrac{2d-1}{2d-2}\left[1-\zeta(30)\right]-\underline{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta R,F}(30)\right)}\leq\frac{\overline{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle c,\Phi}(30)+\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle|\alpha,\Phi|}(30)+\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle|R,\Phi|}(30)}{\left[1-\zeta(30)\right]-\underline{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta R,F}(30)},

which creates a bound that holds uniformly for all
d\geq 30
.

Monotonicity of success for the initialization and improvement of
f_{3}
.

The bound on
f_{3}
is derived in multiple steps, so that we have to check the monotonicity of multiple expressions.
All of these are quite similar and the required monotonicity always follows from the
monotonicity of the NoBLE coefficients of the rewrite and the SRW integrals.

The bounds for the initialization of the bootstrap for
f_{3}
at
z_{I}
is given in
[
12
, (3.30)-(3.31)]
.
To guarantee that we use a monotone bound we omit from
\mathcal{J}_{n,l}(x)
, in
[
12
, (3.30)]

the term
-\tfrac{1}{d}I_{N+3,l}(x)
and use the spatial monotonicity of
I_{n+3,l}(x)
in
|x_{\mu}|

to ensure that we only use values for
I_{n+3,l}(x+2{e}_{\iota})
for which
|x+2{e}_{\iota}|_{\infty}\leq 2
.
This results in a uniform bound on
f_{3}(z_{I})
.

The bound on
f_{3}(z)
for
z\in(z_{I},z_{c})
is stated in
[
12
, (3.87)]

and consists of many individual terms derived in the lines
[
12
, (3.60)-(3.85)]
.
All these terms consists of simple products of bounds on the terms of the rewrite and SRW integrals,
which, as argued before, are monotone decreasing in
d
.

To remove any doubt about monotonicity, we redefine some bounding coefficients whose definition included a factor

\tfrac{2d-2}{2d-1}
:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha_{{\scriptscriptstyle F},z}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1-\zeta(30)}:=\underline{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle\alpha,F}^{-1},

\displaystyle\underline{K}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta F}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{1-\zeta(30)-\underline{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta R,F}(30)},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=\Gamma_{2}.

This results in a bound in which each individual piece is monotone decreasing in
d
,
so that the bound on
f_{3}
holds uniformly in
d\geq 30
.

B.5
Conclusion for all
d\geq 30

Our aim was that to show that our results, as stated in Theorem
1.1
, Corollary
1.3
, Theorem
1.4
and Theorem
1.5
,
hold for all
d\geq d^{\prime}=30
.

The proof of these results is described in Section
2
. Regarding the proof we note that Propositions
2.1
and
2.2
hold
regardless of the dimension, even for
d\leq 16
. Our restriction to
d\geq 16
for LT and
d\geq 17
for LA, respectively,
is only necessary as Proposition
2.3
does not hold in smaller dimensions.

In Appendix
B.4
we conclude a bound on the bootstrap functions that holds uniformly for
d\geq d^{\prime}
.
Using our numerical verification in
LAmonotone
, we check that Proposition
2.3
holds using (only) our monotone bounds. In other words in
d^{\prime}=30
our bootstrap was successful.
As the bounds on the bootstrap function are uniform in
d
we know that Proposition
2.3
therefore also holds for
d^{\prime}\geq d
. For this reason, our analysis succeeds in all
d\geq d^{\prime}=30
.
∎

Appendix C
Formal definition of building blocks

In this section we provide the formal definition of the building blocks described in Section
5.1
.
We begin with the blocks used in the bound without spatial weights as shown in Figure
16

This means, we define intermediates blocks
A^{m,0}(0,v,x,x)
and as initial piece of the diagram
P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})},m},P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})},\iota,m}
for
\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}}_{z}
and
\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota}_{z}
, respectively.
Then, we define the weighted analogue
C^{m,l}
and
\Delta^{\text{start},m}

\Delta^{\text{iota},{\scriptscriptstyle I},m}
,
\Delta^{\text{iota},{\scriptscriptstyle I},m}
.

The blocks are designed to bound the NoBLE coefficients. We define them using as many avoidance/repulsiveness properties of the coefficients as possible, to create the sharpest numerical bounds. To give a visual example, we define
A^{0,-2}
,
A^{0,-1}
,
A^{0,0}
,
A^{0,1}
,
A^{0,-2}
using Tables
3
-
7
. In these tables, we consider various different cases, we give a visual representation of the bounding diagram for the respective case, and give the corresponding bounding diagrams in formulas in the right column. Let us illustrate this by the example
A^{0,-1}
, which we define using Table
6
as the sum of the elements in the right-most column:

\displaystyle A^{0,-1}(0,v,x,y)=\delta_{0,v}\Big(\mathscr{T}_{\underline{1},\underline{1},2}(x,y,0)+\sum_{w}\big(\mathscr{S}_{\underline{1},\underline{1},1,1}(x,y,w,0)+\mathscr{S}_{2,\underline{1},0,0}(x,y,w,0)\big)\Big),

where
\delta_{0,v}
denotes the Kronecker delta.
For our definitions we use translation variance to simplify the presentation, e.g., we use

\displaystyle A^{m,l}(u,v,x,y)=A^{m,l}(0,v-u,x-u,y-u),

and we restrict to defining
A^{m,l}(0,v,x,y)
for
m,l\in\{-2,-1,0,1,2\}
.

Intermediate diagrams
A^{m,l}
and
\bar{A}^{m,l}

Here we formally define the bounding diagrams
A^{m,l}
and
\bar{A}^{m,l}
as informally described in Section
5.1
.

A^{m,l}(0,v,x,y)
for
m,l\in\{-2,-1,0,1,2\}
.

–

define
A^{0,l}
for all values of

.

We next define

\displaystyle A^{-2,-2}(0,v,x,y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{w}\mathscr{S}_{1,2,0,0}(x,y,w,v).

We define the remaining
A^{m,l}
by the symmetries, for
m\in\{1,2\}
,

\displaystyle A^{m,0}(0,v,x,y)=A^{-m,0}(0,v,x,y),

and, for
m,l\in\{1,2\}
,

\displaystyle A^{m,l}(0,v,x,y)=A^{m,-l}(0,v,y,x).

Definition of
\bar{A}^{m,l}(0,v,x,y)
for
m,l\in\{-2,-1,0,1,2\}
.

Next, we define the double open diagram
\bar{A}^{m,l}(0,v,x,y)
, to which the connection
x
to
y
, as present in
A^{m,l}(0,v,x,y)
, does not contribute.
For
l=0
, this connection is not present in
A^{m,l}(0,v,x,y)
either, as
x=y
, so we simply define, for
m\in\{-2,-1,0,1,2\}
,

\displaystyle\bar{A}^{m,0}(0,v,x,y)=A^{m,0}(0,v,x,y),

and use symmetry to define

\displaystyle\bar{A}^{0,m}(0,v,x,y)=A^{m,0}(x,y,0,v).

Further, we define
\bar{A}^{-1,-1},\bar{A}^{-1,-2}
and
\bar{A}^{-2,-2}
as

\displaystyle\bar{A}^{-1,-1}(0,v,x,y)=

\displaystyle(2d)^{2}D(v)D(x-y)\tilde{G}_{1,z}(x)\sum_{w}\mathscr{B}_{0,0}(w-v,x-v),

\displaystyle\bar{A}^{-1,-2}(0,v,x,y)=

\displaystyle 2dD(v)\tilde{G}_{1,z}(x)\sum_{w}\mathscr{B}_{0,0}(w-v,x-v),

\displaystyle\bar{A}^{-2,-2}(0,v,x,y)=

\displaystyle\tilde{G}_{1,z}(x)\sum_{w}\mathscr{B}_{0,0}(w-v,x-v),

and define the remaining diagrams by

\displaystyle\bar{A}^{m,l}(0,v,x,y)

\displaystyle=\bar{A}^{m,-l}(0,v,y,x),\qquad\bar{A}^{m,l}(0,v,x,y)=\bar{A}^{-m,l}(0,v,y,x),

\displaystyle\bar{A}^{m,l}(0,v,x,y)

\displaystyle=\bar{A}^{l,m}(x,y,0,v).

for
m,l\in\{-2,-1,1,2\}
for which
\bar{A}^{m,l}
was not yet defined.

C.2
Initial diagrams of
\Xi
and
\Xi^{\iota}

In this section, we define the bounds on the initial diagrams of
\Xi
and
\Xi^{\iota}
.

C.2.1
Initial diagrams of
\Xi

Here we define the left-most part of the bounding diagram,
P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})},m}
, for
\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})}}
. This diagram represent the first piece of the rib-/sausage-walk, until the first self-intersection. For LTs we simply defined
P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})},m}(x,y)
to be equal to
A^{0,m}(0,0,x,y)
. The LAs can contain an additional triangle, created by the case
\underline{b}_{1}\neq 0
, as discussed in Section
4.2

(recall also Definition
3.3
). To include this case, we define, for LAs, recalling also Definition
4.7
,

\displaystyle S^{0}(x,y)=

\displaystyle\delta_{x,0}\delta_{y,0}+\delta_{x,y}\mathscr{D}_{1}(x),

\displaystyle S^{-1}(x,y)=

\displaystyle\delta_{y,0}\mathscr{B}_{\underline{1},3}(x,0)+(1-\delta_{y,0})\mathscr{T}_{1,\underline{1},1}(x,y,0),

\displaystyle S^{-2}(x,y)=

\displaystyle\delta_{y,0}\mathscr{D}_{2}(0)+(1-\delta_{y,0})\mathscr{T}_{1,2,1}(x,y,0).

Using this notation we define

\displaystyle P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})},m}(x,y)=\sum_{l=-2}^{0}\sum_{u,v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}S^{l}(u,v)A^{l,m}(u,v,x,y).

C.2.2
Initial diagrams of
\Xi^{\iota}

Now we define
P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})},\iota,m}
that bounds the left-most diagram of
\Xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota}
. These bounds are also used for
\Pi^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm N})},\iota,\kappa}
.
Due to the constraint that either
{e}_{\iota}\in A_{0}
or
{e}_{\iota}=\overline{b}_{1}
, this diagram can have many different forms.
For a clear definition we group them into
16
groups that we denote by
B^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm M})},m}
for
M=1,\dots,16
, depicted in Figure
20
,
and define

\displaystyle P^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm 1})},\iota,m}(x,y)=\sum_{M=1}^{16}B^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm M})},m}(x,y).

For
M\geq 5
the diagrams for LTs are trivial, i.e.
B^{{\scriptscriptstyle(\text{\rm M})},m}=0
for
M\geq 5
, as they require a double connection from
0
to
\underline{b}_{1}
in the first rib/sausage, which is not possible for LTs.
